HAIR ORNAMENTS.

The 'newest hair ornament consists of a bunch of miniature golden quills caught with a jewelled buckle, or with a tiny rosette of gold-coloured velvet. In some cases these quills are almost covered with a species of mail, consisting of superposed gold paillettes, so arranged as to over-lap each other. Bunches of gold or silver foliage are treated in the same manner with capital effect

===== 0002601_19070504_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SMART COLOUR:

Yellow is to be the fashionable colour this sea- son, London having caught the yellow craze from Paris, where a sulphur shade is in vogue. Not only for frocks and frills but for home de- coration, says the Taller, yellow is the reigning tint in many shades, from pale primrose to saf- fron. The yellow craze is a repetition of a pre- vious one which prevailed twenty years ago.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
To-DAY'S RECIPE,

GATEAU CHOCOLAT. Take 3oz. of powdered chocolate, 4oz. of sweet almonds, 4oz. of pow- dered sugar, loz. of butter, six eggs (leaving out the whites of two), a little vanilla essence, some rich short crust, strawberry jam: Blanch the almonds and pound them in a mortar with a little cold water, then put them in a basin. add the sugar, vanilla essence, Chocolate, and yolks of the, eggs, and work all well together with a .wooden spoon for ten minutes. Then add the butter, ‘vhich must be' just warmed. Whisk the whites of the eggs to a stiff froth, and stir very lightly to the mixture. Line a round haling-tiT nice., of rich short crust, spread at the bottom a thin layer ot strawberry jam, pour over the mixture, and bake in a moderately quick\ oven for about three-quarters of an hour. When cold ice the gateau with chocolate icing.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
BALD THROUGH DISEASE.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NE

AN ELECTRIC PLANT.

A plant has been discovered in Nicaragua which appears to be charged with elec- tricity. If one of its branches is touched with the naked hand, a distinct shock is felt similar to that produced by a battery. Its influence upon a magnetic needle is notice- able at a distance of half-a-dozen yards, and as one gets nearer the plant this influence in- creases; while, if the instrument is placed in the centre of the bush, it will assume a deadly circular movement. The intensity of the phenomenon seems to depend entirely upon the time of day.

A FIRE-PROOF BRICK.

A glass company abroad reports the dis- covery of materials and a process of manu- facture which produce a remarkable brick. The chief materials are magnesia and silica rock, which, when ground and mixed with secret ingredients, rapidly hardens in the air, and becomes a non-conductor of heat, cold, and sound. A. sample, 2in. long, • was exposed at one en.d to an intensely hot flame for /ten minutes, and could then be held in the hand by taking hold of the other end. When sub- jected to a strong blow-pipe flame and sud- denly plunged,. in cold water no injury was detected. It is said the cost of making is no greater than the ordinary clay brick.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
ARE CRIMINALS DREAMLESS?

As a result of his investigations of dreams, a specialist informs Science Siftings that children begin to dream before their fourth year, but are unable to recall dreams before the age of four or five. This age, he con- cludeks, is that .at which a child first becomes distinctly conscious of self. Aged persons dream less frequently and less vividly than the young. Women's dreams are more fre- quent, more vivid and better remembered than those of men. Criminals and delin- quents dream much less frequently and much less vividly than other people. Two-thirds of the criminals examined by the scientist quoted were never conscious of dreaming. This, is ascribed to lack of mental activity.

THE EXTRAORDINARY BIRD STOMACH.

Most astonishing things have been found in the stomachs of birds, everything but diamonds, states Professor Beal. "A bird stomach which i had been kept in alcohol for two years waiting its 'turn to be examined, contained poison oak berries, which are the favourite food of many birds. The man who examined this stomach was badly poisoned. Vicious- and deadly-poison spiders constitute a favourite bird food. The mere touch of a blister beetle • would scorch the flesh of a human, yet in the stomach of one king bird fourteen of these _ fiery creatures were dis- coveral. Caterpillars with stinging spines, beetles with acrid secretions that are bitter and burning, .insects with an odour so fierce that a skunk is fragrant in comparison, and fruit bitter and rasping as quinine, and thou- sands of other obnoxious things, are con- sumed greedily, by the feathered throng."

===== 0002601_19070504_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
SENSE OF HEARING IN FISHES.

At a meeting of the French Academy of Sciences, Professor Delage read an account of a series of experiments made by M. Mar- age on the sense of hearing in fishes. Many difilcA ties were encountered in performing accurate experiments. If. a fish be placed in a tank, the sound being reflected from all the walls, the animal having no clue as to the origin of the sound vibrations, • will make no attempt at fleeing. If, however, the fishes are experimented on in the state of liberty, these i'investigations will be found still more diffi- cult. At all events, fishermen do believe in 'the sense of hearing of fishes. Marage used an indiarubber funnel to transmit the sound to the water, ,this funnel being tuned to the number of vibrations of the sound in ques- tion. The vowels a, e, i, o, u, were produced within a range of four octaves, and with the energy generally used with deaf-mutes. The experiments were made on fishes, both at liberty and in captivity. In the latter case, a diver was able to perceive and to analyse the sound vibrations in the water to distances up to 100 ft. Although these vibrAtions failed to shew any effect on • the fishes, M. Marage thinks it quite possible that the animals may hear the noises produced by fishermen. It should, however, be remembered that this rudimentary sense of hearing is compensated by highly-developed senses of touch and vision.

BRAIN WEIGHT AND INTELLECTUALITY.

No question has been more exhaustively discussed with so little result in the shape of a definite settlement, writes Charles H. Cor- bin, than the relation of the size of the brain to the power of the mind. There is, urr doubtedly, a general presumption that a large head means a strong intellect, A dozen familiar phrases testify to the prevalence of the idea; and yet scientists, after much in- vestigation, find no decisive evidence to support it. Some of +he heaviest brains have belonged to individuals of small mental de- velopment. On the other hand, that: of the famous French statesman and orator Gam- betta—a man of brilliant ability and highly emotional temperament astonished the medical world by its lightness, weighing only about 1,100 grams, or less than 40 troy ounces. It is quite clear that quality of brain counts for much more than quantity.

GERMINATIVE POWER OF SEEDS.

Little is known about how long the seeds of the various vegetables preserve their germi- native power. De Candolle alone, hitherto, had studied the question. In 1846 he sowed the seeds of 368 different species gathered by himself and preserved for fourteen years. He saw seventeen species germinate; five mal- vaceous (out of , ten sown) ; nine leguminous (out of forty-five); one labiaceous (out of thirty). Mons. Paul Becquerel took up this investigation upon a larger scale, and has communicated his results to the French Acta- demie des Sciences. He sowed 550 species belonging to thirty families of monocotyle- dons and dicotyledons. These seeds had been preserved at the museum for a time VARYING FROM TWENTY:FIVE TO 135 YEARS. They were washed in sterilised water; husked when the integument appeared too imperme- able; then placed upon moist aseptic coton hydrophile in a glass tank covered with a glass plate, which WaB maintained at a con- stant temperature of 28deg. Cent. Out of these 550 species, each represented by a dozen seeds, twenty-three only germinated: eighteen leguminous (two laburnum, one mimosa, two acacias) • three lotus; one mal- vaceous (lavatera), and one labiate (stachys). Seeds 'from thirty to sixty years old, several of which had the reputation of being good for several centuries, did not germinate. Of this number were: poppy, tobacco, euphorbia, foxglove, &c. The seeds more than forty-five years old that germinated belong to the fol- lowing species: Acacia bicapsularis, Cytisus biflorus, Leucoena leucocephala. These seeds have a thick integument assuring impermea- bility to the gas of the atmosphere almost as perfect as if that impermeability had been nrnriiiePri artifieis,lly. This peculiarity aarees with the fact universally known to-day, tnat desiccation is an indispensable condition in the good preservation of seeds.

LIVERPOOL AND NORTH WALES STEAMBOAT

SERVICE. The Liverpool and North Wales Steamship Company. announce that regular daily sailings (Sundays included) will commence to-day (Saturday), 4th May, when their steamer Snowdon will leave for Llandudno, Beaumaris, and Bangor (where a connection will be made for those passengers proceeding to Menai Bridge, excepting Sun- days). A most delightful trip can be made by the above steamer with fully three hours allowed at Llan- dudno, and returning in time for evening trains to all parts of Lancashire and Yorkshire. The company will continue to supervise the catering department, and passengers may rely upon their wants being attended to at most reasonable prices. Commencing 17th May, the St. Elvies will take up the daily sailings to Llandudno, Beaumaris, Bangor, and Menai Bridge, allowinc, fully four hours ashore at Llandudno, and due back in Liver- pool about 7.30 p.m. Increased sailings are advertised for Whitsuntide holidays.

THE EXPLOITS OF THE I ONE-EYED MAN.

BY EDWARD TOWLER.

lI.—THE AFFAIR OF THE SPECIE VAN: "The Cunard Company's steamship, the ; Lucania, left New York on Saturday with

two hundred thousand pounds specie on board _ _ _

for the Bank of Englaiid."

• Such was the text of a four-line para- ' graph in the shipping column of a London daily paper that the One-eyed Man was read- ing. His single eye shot forth a cat-like gleam as he read the news. " Phew ! Two hundred thousand pounds i Pretty decent, Greaser ! " The Greaser sat by the fire. The. two con- federates were in the smoking-room of a hydro, at Blackpool,eand, being the back end of the season, they were alone. The Greaser took the paper from the hands of his superior and read the paragraph himself. "Well, Boss," he said, "there was a time when I might have thought the joint above your carving, but it's an adobe hut to an estancia that you can do it, though the East Anglian Bank job was child's play -compared with this. I don't ask your plan, for I know it will be good enough; but when in creation will you have a pile big enough to satisfy you? I know you have a fairish bit ' cached ' safely for yourself. My little pile is a cool ten thousand now, and I. think of settling down somewhere in the country, getting made a J.P., and being looked up to as a pillar in rural society. Call the game, guv'nor, what's yours? President of a South American re- public or what?" "Greaser," said the One-eyed, Man, "you give me an idea. It would not be a bad game, and I could do the ruling all right. Then there would be dollars accruing. I will think it over. But to business. The Lucania left New York last Saturday. She will be due at Liverpool on Saturday. Specie unshipped first, naturally. The specie-boat train should leave for London about four; Nuneaton, say, six. They slack up through there, and a mile or two beyond is a gradient. The average speed there will not be more than thirty miles an hour; that is one point. Get the map." The Greaser got the map, asking. no .ques- tions, for at a time .when the One-eyed Man's brain was working at high pressure he brooked no interference. The map was opened, and both studied it intently for some time.

At Ipst the One-eyed Man spoke, "Make note," he said. "Wire Paul to come at once. Say in the telegram, 'ln answer to your ad- vertisement in Daily Telegraph call—Miller Hydro, Blackpool, to-morrow, noon.' Take it to the post-office yourself." Ten minutes later the Greaser returned. "Let me see," said the One:eyed Man, "to-day is Wednesday, and it is now 11.30. You must start at once for Liverpool. There's a train from the Central station at twelve o'clock. On arriving at Liverpool you must find out what lights.will be on the tail van of the special express to Ruston from the Riverside Station. Take mental notes—no scribbling, as people might notice you—of any other par- ticulars likely to be of use to us, and meet me here again to-night." The Greaser saluted, and, turning on hie heel, left the room. The. One-eyed Man con- tinued in a brown stud. His fingers locked and unlocked, while the end of the cigar he was smoking glowed like .a furnace under the strong pulls he was taking. After an interval of an hour he rose from his chair, and, throw- ing the stump of his cigar in the grate, rant-, the bell, ordere.d a "Scotch and Polly," and, the problem he had been studying being solved, enjoyed it to the full.. The Greaser returned in the evening, and together they compared notes. It's a green light, Boss, on the last van. There will be twc) vans—r specie an and a I,7ange van." "So much the better,- said the One-eyeL• Jan. "They won't particularly notice a vaz 0 the train for a mile or two. The Panharki oust be at Shilton Colliery siding gates at 5.15 p.m. on Saturday. We shall unhitch thk, .van between Buckington and Shilton, ane switch it down the disused colliery siding,. The points must be well oiled. Then we shaP unload on to the car and make tracks for-- well, its destination I leave. Suffice it that it *will not be ten miles away. We dare not hold that amount of stuffqeng,' but its resting- place will surprise even you, Greaser." "I trust you, Boss," laughed the Greaser.. "Yours is the master mind."

Morning ,brought a member of the gang named Paul, and his instructions were brief nd to the point. "Paul," said the Ore-eyed Man, "your business is to ,o to the rendezvous, and give this letter to Bresei and Orloff. They are to have the Panhard at the .-gates of the Shilton Colliery siding—it is disused now—at 6.15 p.m., on Saturday. They will see a passenger van come down the siding, and they must be ready. Your duty is to see that the switch points are in borough working order, and the moment after you see the train go past close the points, for our van will be travelling be< hind the mail, and only just uncoupled. You must be quick, Paul, for if you fail me we lose the boodle and you your job." "Right, Boss. Anything further? " • "No, nothing. Remember upon your quick- ness depends five thousand pounds for your- self, if all goes well. If not, you yourself will stand in no Small jeopardy." "Look here, Boss,' said. Paul, "don't you fear. Didn't I manage the job of the State Express on the Grand Trunk? Well, I smile. SWitch a van! Snakes! it's a flea-bite." "Right, Paul, you're the man -for my money." With a brief good-night Paul left. "Now, Greaser, there iS your part in this affair. You must secure a green lamp for the tail of the train, for . every signalman knows a train by, its tail lights, and if these were missing, at the first cabin passed the signal- man would put his long-distance signals against , it or telegraph on, and the train would be pulled up. When yo fi have procured a lamp you will reserve the last carriage on the special for yourself and a friend who is ex- pected by the Lucania. We shall thus have the carriage to ourselves and be free from in- terruption. After that there is nothing further to be done until the time fkr action arrives. There will he two men travelling in the specie van. You must' . tackle one, I the other. By the way,l regret it may be neces= sary to shoot the- guard as we crawl past .his window, for if he missed the van it would be all U.P. with us. However, that is a detail, except for the guard.:' The Greaser humraed a tune from the Beggar's Opera, and built castles in Mexico with his share. "The guard?. Oh, yes. Well, it's in his day's work, and he must take it as it comes. • Collision might kill him any day, Boss." "Greaser, you are a philosopher. - I did not know that trait in your many-sided character." Saturday came, and the One-eyed Man • and the Greaser, both disguised, had obtained access to the departure 'platform at, the River- side Station. After carefully noting the coup- ling of the specie van they got into .the re- served compartment next the. luggage van just as the guard blew his -whistle, and the train moved out of the station. " Hurrah !" 'said the Greaser, "4.10. Plenty of time. Here's the lamp." Opening his bag; he took out a railwayilamp similar to the one on the tail of the train. It was fully prepared—wick, oil, and everything complete. "Now," said the One-eyed Man, when he had eXamined it, "here is -my lifting-jack. You will see its- principle is that used for lift- ing derailed engines. You slip this under the coupling and pump." "Do I crawl out for the job?" asked. the Greaser. - "No," said the One-eyed Man, "we cut a hole with this saw and put the clutch through. The thing is dead easy. We will commence operations after we. have passed Stafford." Stafford was left behind, and when well out the station the Boss sprang to his feet. "Now ! " he said. While the train was in dark of the country they slinned out on to the footboards of the van. The One-eyed Man cleared his arm for action, and although the terrifid force of the wind

and the vibration of the train made his pro- gress a perilous undertaking, the iron nerve of the man never flinched. The Greaser, clutching the tail lamp, left the compart- ment by the opposite door. Creeping along the footboard, one on either side, the two reached the luggage van, and the Greaser, climbing the steps at the end of the van, 'fixed the lamp he carried in.to the socket, without attracting the attention of the guard. Then, creeping along, they got to the window of the specie van. They gaVe a sharp rap on the glass, and simultaneously two heads appeared. The pistols carried by the con- federates sped their deadly messengers, and the two guardians of the bullion fell limp and lifeless on the floor of the van. Letting themselves in by the aid of their pass-keys, the One-eyed Man spoke: "So far, excellent. Are they dead?" pointing to the two men on the floor. The Greaser bent over them. "Stone dead, Boss," replied the man. "I'll put 'em in the corner out of the way," and he carried them to the other end of the van.

The One-eyed Man was gloating over the haul before him. There were twenty boxes of minted sovereigns and dollars, all his, and the murder of two more men upon his soul—but he reeked little of that. "Now, Greaser, out w4th that saw, and cut a hole a. foot in circumTerence ; never mind the noise—the train drowns that." Five minutes was sufficient to cut out the panel- ling, and the measurement had been correctly •alaulated, for when the piece fell out, it was only nine inches above the coupling. The handing . the clutch-hook to the Greaser, who dropped it through the coupling-chain, the One-eyed Man got the jack to work, and found` all ir, order. "Where are we, Greaser?" he asked, I. sharply. "Nuneaton, Boss "—as a station flashed by. " Good ! " said the other. "Five miles more to Shilton. We can travel a mile -under our own impetus. What rate do you think • • we are running now?" " Sixty, Boss." "Out with your watch, now; tell me when two minutes have gone." The Greaser, watch in hand, counted the seconds as they went by. "Time," he said. Then the other, seizing the handle of the jack, wonked it vigorously, while the Greaser reported progress. • "It's coming—it's off, Boss ! " There was a clang as the chain slipped back and hung limp. but the roar of the train drowned the noise. For a minute the "way "on the van kept it well up with the train, and then the dis- tame widened between them. Outside the night was black as ink. "By Heaven," ejaculated the One-eyed Man, "if Paul fails us ! " At that moment a lurch took the van with a wrench that nearly caused it to leave the metals, and threw them both down. The One-eyed Man 'sprang up. "Quick, on with the brakes; it's Shilton siding that Paul's switched us over." The -Greaser threw off the lock-chain and put on the hand-brake, and then the van, run- ning down the disused siding, pulled up in as quiet a spot as one could wish. On the country lane that ran alongside the line stood the powerful Panhard. with Bresci and Orloff in readiness. Ostensibly built for eight persons, the whole interior of .the motor, as Bresei described it, was built for cargo. To transfer the bullion was the work of minutes only, and Paul, who had joined ,them, was heartily greeted both by the One-eyed Man and the Greaser. "I add a thousand to your vhare. Paul," said the former; " y9u did well.

Paul drawled out in inimitable American, "I guess the old cayuse nearly bucked you out when she took the hurdle."

The •One-eyed Man laughed. "We had her on the snaffle, Paul. Now let's away; the van can be left here." "The rendezvous, Boss?" said the Greaser. "Where do we make for?" "The canal, seven miles away," was the re- ply. "You know, Bresci. You had your in- structions. Is all ready?"

"All is ready, Bcss," answered the other, with a smile. "Then go ahead !" The Panhard leaped into the night, and •ifteen minutes had reached the canal. Aa old canal boat was moored to the side, with. a cargo of oileake and bides: It was in charge of two men, who wore the blue jerseys typical of their class. These were two of the crew of the Submarine.

The One-eyed Man looked at the Greaser, who was lost in admiration.

" Boss scheme.

' he said, "it's a daddy of a Where's she bound for?"

-" She can go from here to London, Greaser, and in London river we have our Sub, marine."

So it came about that, under its pile of cake and hides, a dingy canal-boat carried two hundred - thousand pounds in minted coin; without doubt the costliest cargo that ever canal -bo a t carried dawn the sluggish reaches of ca;ml and river, in safety, while England was scoured for the missing bullion. The One-eyed Man and the Greaser were taken to within a mile of Stafford, where they left the motor and proceeded on foot to the railway station, an .1 with tickets marked Euston to Dublin, that Bres,2i had brought with him from London, the two men boarded the night mail to Holyhead. Bresei and Or- loff, posing as . independent gentleman on a pleasure tour, went on to Chester and North Wales.

The van was not missed until the train ar- rived at Euston. Then a great he and cry was raised. The newly-greased points and switch gave a clue to the whereabouts of the van when daylight came, and the discovery at Shilton siding shewed the enormity of another mysterious crime the second of. a long list which made the country wild with indignation, and terrorised the more timid section of the population. Meanwhile, Scotland Yard was not idle, and the story of the first encounter between an official of the Criminal Investigation De- partment and the One-eyed Man made, thrilling reading when, some time later on, it appeared in the papers. •

"And have you had an enjoyable day's fishz ing, dear?" asked the fond wife, as her husband returned in the evening and proudly displayed six trout in his basket. Yes, there was no doubt about it, Mr. Hunter had lost the "field." He had searched for th& other fox-hunters long but vainly, and he was now reduced to seeking aid of a chubby little lad' of three, whom he met in a. lane. "Hullo, Johnny, which way did the hounds go?" he queried. "Johnny" dropped his gaze, and appeared too frightened to answer. "Come," coaxed Mr. Hunter, "don't be afraid; here's a pnrly for you. Now, tell me, which way did the hounds go?" The youngster took the coin, and then fell upon all fours and imitating the barking of dogs, "Dakway," he said, shyly.

The graceless young bachelor's tongue was prettLy protuding between. his lips; his arms were squared; h was diligently guiding a pen over a lilywhite sheet before him, pausing anoli to look up a dictionary and to take a draught of the plain soda-water which stood on the table. Attending school many years ago was a boy who gave the teacher much annoyance by his un- tidiness. His hair was seldom combed, and his face as seldom washed. One morning he appeared dirtier than usual, and the teacher, by way of contrast, called him out to the front, and placed him side by side with another boy who was clean and tidy. "Now, William, said the teacher, "why cannot you come to school with as clean face and hands as Johnnie here?" "Please, sir," said William, "he bides nearer a pump than me."

L

The Examiner, Saturday, May 4, 1907.

habit of asking for a " sub" each day is to a certain extent lowering his dignity and taking away that self-independence which every man ought to cultivate. Some of them play upon your sympathies by saying that they have nothing to eat and they can- not work without food, when at the same time their very breath tells you that they have made a choice between drink and food, and if drink is not a food then why does the working man give his hard-earned. money in exchange for it? A " sub " to this man is helping him to starvation. These men are to be seen in groups, sitting in open spaces or round the corner of off-licensed houses.lf they could only see themselves as others see them and feel the disgust which the passer-by must feel when he hears their profane language, they would be men and stand apart from an influence which is daily making them poorer. An "odd man" or "bye tarn" man who cannot demand a "sub" will borrow sixpence from a steady workman and pay him back at the week-end. If this is refused in the hope that the man will be able to redeem himself if he will wait and draw his wage in full, then he pursues his request with a bold determination, will often follow his man home and when throu&h his impor- tunity he has received the coin he has asked for, in many cases it is given in payment for drink. Can anything be done to reclaim these men and to bring them back to their normal state of manhood? May the young men who are growing up take a look into the future and ask themselves this question • , What profit is there in leading such a life? Will the steady men in our works use their influence and do I all they can to raise the moral life of their fellow- workmen? •

BE MEN. NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Communications vflOrn STANLEY LORD and LAZARUS held over till next eek.

THE PALACE THEATRE AND HIPPODROME. Laying of the Foundation Stone. INTERESTING SPEECHES. Under the happiest auspices the ceremony of laying the foundation stone of the new Palace Theatre and Hippodrome was performed by the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) on Wednesday afternoon, in the presence of a large and representative gathering It was indeed a matter for congratulation that, following the depressing conditions of the previous day,the weather was beautifully fine, the sun shining genially upon the day's proceedings, and the undoubted success of the function should be a happy augury for the prosperity of the enterprise which is being regarded with so much interest by the townspeople. Already the building is rising with remarkable rapidity, Mr. C. W. Davenport having been entrusted with the contract. Mr. Francis W. Macnaghten, who will be the managing director, is at present the proprietor, lessee or managing director of the following theatres in England : —The Palace Theatre, Blackburn; the Palace Theatre, Bradford; the Palace Theatre, Bath - the Palace Theatre, Lincoln; the Palace Theatre, Halifax ; the Palace Theatre, Southampton; the Theatre Royal, Leeds ; the Surrey Theatre, London; the Forresters' Theatre, London ; Sadlers Wells Theatre, London; and of theatres at Leicester, Nottingham, Attercliffe, Chesterfield,Rotherham, etc., etc. This important com- bination must of course be of great advantage in the successful running of the theatre at Warrington,which it is intended to open to the public either in August or September. At the invitation of Mr. F. W. Macnaghten and his co-directors, there was a large attendance at the Friars' Gate site for the foundation stone laying ceremony. Amongst those present were :—The Mayor and Mayoress and Miss Smethurst, the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis), Rev. Father Byrne, Mr. F. W. Macnaghten, Mr. H. E. Barnes, Aldermen Francomb, Evans, Forshaw, and Bennett, Councillors Sankey. Steel and Toole, Messrs. J. Lyon Whittle; Roger C. Parr, W. B. Roberts, R. R. Black, J. H. Clarke, E. Bradshaw, Segar Owen,W.Rogers, E. Aukland, W. Rogerson, J. E. Warren,John Lee, ELM. Thorburn (United Theatres, Manchester), F. Wilmot (Liverpool), W. Dalton (London), Steele (Burnley), E. Harrison(Halifax),W.Murray(Palace Theatre,Blackburn) C. F'. Lawton, E. P. Lawton,T. Garton,and H.Singleton. The band of the Bth and 40th Regimental District from Orford Barracks played choice selections. Mr. 11fACNA.GlitEN said the ceremony of laying the foundation stone of what was going to be a very beautiful building would be performed by the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) who had kindly consented to officiate. He would take the opportunity of introducing the architeet, Mr. Ward, of Birmingham, to his Worship. Mr. WARD said he had great pleasure in presbnting to the Mayor a silver trowel. He trusted that when the building was erected it would be a credit to the town which his Worship so worthily represented. Of that, personally he (Mr. Ward) had no fear, so long as they had Mr. Macnaghten at the head of affairs. Mr. Macnaghten had had a very wide experience in the management of theatres and music halls and he had the reputation of always placing before the public enter- tainments which never raised a blush to the cheeks of the most fastidious. Nir.Ward then handed to the Mayor a very handsome trowel suitably inscribed. The MAYOR said he was extremely obliged to Mr. Ward for presenting him with that most beautiful trowel. He was sure that under the management of Mr. Macnaghten, Mr. Barnes and the other directors the entertainments would be such that no-one need be ashamed to take his wife and daughters there. He had had the pleasure of attending one of the performances when Mr. Barnes first came to Warrington and he was delighted with it. When he got home he told his family that if all the performances in the new buildin,, were to be of that character, no one need be ashamed to go to them. He was quite satisfied that Warrington would appreciate that most beautiful building. (Hear, hear.) He thanked them for the beautiful present they had given him and he could assure them he would highly esteem it. There was a little competition in these matters, becanae the Mayoress had already been pre- sented with a trowel for laying the foundation stone of St. Paul's vestry. They would now be equal, in each having a trowel. (Laughter and applause.) The MAYOR then formally laid the stone,which bears the inscription :—" This stone was laid by Alderman J. Smethurst, Esq., J.P., Mayor of Warrington. Ist May, 1907." Amid applause, his Worship declared the stone to be "well and truly laid." Mr. MACNAGRTEN said he had to thank the Mayor for so very kindly performing the ceremony. He par- ticularly wished to thank him for the kind things he had said about himself. He could only say he would in the days to come do his very best to carry out all his Worship had said with regard to the probabilities of his entertainments here. He also wished to thank the Mayoress, Canon Willis, and Father Byrne for their presence, and he could assure them he would do his best to provide entertainments of a class which he thought I would be acceptable in Warrington. He was, he thought, pretty.well known in Lancashire. and York- shire. He had been successful in other places, and with their help and good word, he would be successful here.l (Applause). The playing of the National Anthem concluded the ceremony. LUNCHEON AT THE LION HOTEL.

---"•EHE CHURCH AND THE STAGE. After the cere.nony luncheon was served at the Lion Hotel, Mr.Macnaghten presiding over a large gathering. An excellent repast was served, Mr. Lee catering in his best style. The military band played selections during luncheon in creditable style. The loyal toasts were honoured on the proposition of the Chairman. Alderman FRANCOMB then proposed "Success to the Palace Theatre and Hippodrome" and expressed is pleasure that the day's proceedings had been so enjoy- able and that the weather had favoured them. He was sure all present would wish the new theatre every success. (Applause.). It would be a grand thing for Warrington to have a palace and hippodrome which would no doubt supply a want that had been long felt. They did not wish to decry in any way the Public Hall which was presided over by Mr. Potter for whom he had a very great respect. At the ' same time there was certainly room for Mr. Macnaghten and his friends as well as for Mr. Potter. (Applause.) He firmly believed that instead of injuring Mr. Potter the new place of amusement would mak. the people of Warrington stay in their own town and not dream of going to Manchester and Liverpool. He was thankful to see that they had their beloved Rector with them—(apolause) and also Father Byrne. They knew that those two gentlemen would not be present to uphold a bad scheme and there was no doubt that if they wanted to make the people sober, sensible and happy they must provide amusement for them. The old fashioned Puritan style was for the father to get drunk on Saturday and every night of the week but Sunday, and to read any kind of paper except on Sunday, while the children were very miserable beings and Sunday was looked upon as the worst day of the week. All that was passing away. If they wanted to make children good and happy they must provide some simple and innocent amusement for them, even on a Sunday. He was certain the Rector was most anxious to improve the people in every way and he had no doubt that Father' Byrne sha.ed that view. If they treated the children and the grown-up people as reasonable beings and set them a bright example, there was no reason why the Palace and Hipprodrome and similar entertainments should not be elevating and not demoralisiog. If there was anything demoralising about these places, all they had to do was to use the pulpit to influence the people, and he was quite certain that the theatres could not live in face of the people. He hoped the Palace and Hippodrome would be a wonderful success and the means not only of profit to the promoters but whole- some recreation to the people of Warrington. (Applause.) The RECTOR said it was not without hesitation that he accepted from Mr. Macnaghten and hr. Barnes the kind invitation for that day's proceedings—and he thought with reason, because he felt that in his position great responsibility rested upon whatever he did and said. There were dangers and evils connected with all , amusements and recreations. There was first of all the danger of very late hours unfitting the people for their work the next day. There was a danger that money which perhaps could be ill-afforded might be spent, and- of a low tone instead of a high tone. These were points he felt bound to consider but against them he placed several other considerations. First, I the people must have and would have recreation and amusement and therefore it was their wisdom and duty to try and provide innocently for that. Secondly, he had always believed that the stage was and might be a very great power. When he was at school and college he read the Greek plays of the great tragedians Aeschylus, Sophocles and Euripides, and when he was older he from time to time went to the theatres and no one had enjoyed a good play more than he had. He had been abroad a great deal and he had seen that wonderful Passion Pltey at Ober Ammergau,and

he never realized until he had seen it the depth of meaning of the great events of Holy Week and Good Friday. Therefore he felt that the stage might be, and he hoped would be,an increasingly great power for good. Thirdly he felt it was the bounden duty of the clergy to try and support in everything that was proper and right actois and actresses. If they turned their backs upon them, how could they expect a high tone? A little encouragement and sympathy on the part of the clergy might be the means of doing a real good. (Applause.) The Rev. Father BYRNE said he would not like to refrain from speakine'on that occasion, because it was an occasion on which the clergy should raise their voices on behalf of a very good institution in the town. He had a particular reason for being pleased, because he was an ex-military -nan and be had the keys of the green room in college in his possession for five years and a half. He had the pleasure of getting up Shakes- pearian plays, melo-dramas and other things in college and had many pleasing recollections of those days. Having given a number of interesting reminiscences, Father Byrne said he always thought with Shakespeare that they should" hold the mirror up to nature," and , show nature in her fairest forms. He believed that nneer the management of Mr. Macnaghten and his co-directors they need not only have no fear but they could look with an assured hope to an improving morality on the stage. (Applause.) Shakespeare said : " The man that hath no music in himself, Nor is not moved with concord of sweet sounds, ' Is fit for treasons, stratagems and spoils." Let him go to the public house, but let the wise man go to the theatre and see nature reflected in her fairest forms. He gave his hearty assent and approval to that movement in Warrington. From his inmost heart he did approve of the stage, as the grandest thing with the pulpit for the morality of our people. A good ulay was a magnificent object lesson for young and old alike. The people saw in the actor the embodiment of all that was most noble in the human character, when properly portrayed. They must have—it could not he otherwise in the course of things—inferior articles thrust upon them, but when they had the assurance of such men as Mr. Macnaghten and his co-directors, he did not think if a syndicate of angels came down to one of their entertainments, they would do anything but applaud with their wings. (Laughter and applause.) He wished the new venture a prosperous career (Applause). The toast having been enthusiastically honoured, Mr. MACNAGHTEN said he might explain, in answer to a auestion from Canon Willis, that the reason he called his entertainment houses palaces and hippodromes was that he wished as far as possible to distinguish them altogether from the old music hal Ls of which people talked, and which unfortunately in the old days were of rather bad repute. The entertainment places of the present day, though sometimes spoken of as music halls, I were altogether different from the music halls they heard • of when they were boys. In those days the chief attrac- tion was the bar, the entertainment being subsidiary and not conducted like entertainments were how. At present they had their bars, because people I required refreshment, and they could be supplied in i reasonable quantities, but they practically gave no attention to tteit, as far as pushing the business of the bar was concerned. Their province was the stage and they made it their business to bring to the towns in England where it was their privilege to cater, the best that the world produced. When he said the world, he meant it, because they had to ransack the Continent and I America to find novelities. His experience had been that, producing as he did a complete change of enter- tainment every week at twenty different houses, it was necessary to go abroad to find the change of talent to simply the programme. As regarded what he proposed doing in Warrington he would condense it into one sentence : "I will do that which is right." (Applause.) He was hoping in.the days to come to have the pleasure of standing before them as one of their friends to receive their congratulations. He was not altogether unknown in Lancashire and Yorkshire, having many houses in the vicinity. He had done his best and having been successful in pleasing the people, having never had a row with the police, and having that day received the promise of help from the pulpit, he hoped the success of the enterprise in Warrington would be greater than that which had attended any other theatre with which he had been associated. He would do his best and he thought they would be pleased. (Applause.) Mr. E. P. LA.WTON proposed the health of the Mayor and said his Worship in laying the foundation stone of the new theatre had performed an office which was worthy of his position as head of the municipality. It had been acknowledged that day that amusement was necessary for the people and therefore the establish- ment of a good musio hall was for the benefit of the community. He thought they would find that the rise of the modern music hall had been the fall of the low public house. The drinking habits of the people had been diminished not simply by the outpourings of the rabid teetotalers but because they had found in the music hall the necessary amusement. The Mayor could not have distinguished himself more than by signalising his approval of such an undertaking. (Applause.) Mr. H. E. BARNICS said he could supplement what Mr. Lawton had said from the fact that he had had personal experience of the Mayor, Aldermen and Coun- cillors of Warrington. He had never found a kinder and better-hearted gentleman than their worthy Mayor and he was sure his Worship would give,them all the help he could in their enterprise. Everyone had appre- ciated his presence and they hoped to have him with them at the opening day. (Applause.) The toast was honoured with great enthusiasm. The MAYOR in responding, said the Palace Theatre would be a new feature in the annals of Warrington. He hoped it would be for the benefit of the working class and the middle class—in fact of all classes—to have a place of amusement equal to those in Manchester and Liverpool, for it would certainly save the patrons much expense. With the advancement of civilization a great change had come over their views. In the old times they used to be told that they must do such a thing and nothing else. In the present enlightened age the progress of education had brought out the faculties of the people, and they required recreation after the studies in which they were engaged in their various spheres. Relaxation was necessary, and such maguifl- cent theatres as the one of which he had laid the' foundation stone would provide it. From the informa- tion he had procured he was satisfied that he was not disgracing his chain of office by taking part in that function. He believed the new theatre would not only be a place of amusement but that it would elevate and instruct the people. Mr. Potter and those associated with him had done their best in the past, and he was sure they would continue to do so, but they all knew that competition would have the effect of bringing the best talent to Warring- ton. (Applause). The finances of the town would doubtless be benefited, for people would come in from the outlying districts to see the entertainments at both theatres. lie hoped at some future gathering Mr. Macnaghten would have the satisfaction of declaring a dividend of sor even 10 per cent. (Applause). Mr. C. F. LAWTON proposed the health of Mr. Ward, the architect. The inauguration of an enterprise like the new theatre was, he said, fraught with cares and anxieties, but in Mr. Ward they had an expert who had had great experience in the erection of theatres in all parts of the country. To him (the speaker) as secretary of the company, it was a great comfort to know that they were in the hands of such a capable man who would see them through all difficulties. Judging from the plans, the theatre would mean the addition of a build- ing of great beauty to Warrington, and one of which the Mayor and every burgess would be rightly proud. Mr. WARD in responding said he only hoped that when the building was erected, they would not be dis- appointed. The position of a theatrical architect was no sinecure, for he was run after by proprietors with red hot irons telling him to hurry up or they would be losing £lOO a week. (Laughter). He (the speaker) had the reputation that he had never disappointed his clients when he had given them a date when the theatre should be opened—(upplause)—and he had built twenty or thirty theatres in different parts of the country. He had been associated with various kinds of builders and contractors, but the manner in which Vessrs. Davenport and Sons had carried out the work at Warrington up to the present time had somewhat paralysed him. They were absolutely the smartest and most up-to-date con- tractors he had come into contact with. Their werk was excellently done and he had no hesitation in saying that the building would be completed u•iless there were earthquakes or similar happenings—(laughter)—by the advertised date. Mr. Ward concluded by proposing the health of the builders which was heartily drunk.

Mr. C. W. DAVENPORT responded and said he believed instead of giving promises, to let the building, as far as speed and quality went, speak for itself. He was perfectly satisfied that the company with whom he had entered into contract consisted of gentlemen, and they could rely upon him doing his best. (Applause.) He would endeavour to carry out the work expeditiously and in a manner which would be creditable to the town. Mr. W. B. ROBERTS proposed the health of NI r. Macnaghten and said he was a gentleman ot very strong personal character. They were all proud that lie was coming to Warrington to take part in its com- mercial welfare. Nothing was required to show what an able and honourable man he was beyond the invari- able satisfaction which he gave to the public. The toast was most heartily received. Mr. MACNA GHTEN said he was really much touched, for although he had attended many functions of that kind before he had never received such a warm welcome. There was an old saying that actions spoke louder than words and he would do his utmost to merit, the kind things they had said of him. (Applause.) This concluded a highly successful function.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and ' endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. 31.5.5. must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week.

This week, my attenthin has been called to a sermon preached in St. Paul's Church by the Rev. W. Brace- camp. On Sunday last, the reverend gentleman 19 reported to have said: "One of the greatest scandals of the day was the little regard that was paid to genuine honesty. If only it would secure a point, if only it would open a way of escape from a present difficulty, or if some pecuniary advantage might be reaped,and at the same time a man could just keep within the bare letter of the la,a, no scruple was entertained about the course to be taken, and as to whether it be strictly honest or no was reckoned a very secondary consideration. In almost every department of life the same blot might be discovered. On the Stock Exchange the Jew could discern no difference between the pro- fessing Christian and himself in a readiness to take an unfair advantage of another." I have no desire to add fuel to the fire. I am, how- ever, anxious to break down the barriers which separate the lay from the cleric and to look upon him, for the moment, as an ordinary being and not as one privileged of heaven. The honest and intelligent man- in-the-street has been saying exactly the same things for years and the hurch has been passive—quietly rocking herself to sleep —whilst the people have been following the devices and desires of their own hearts. Do material things, after all, .count as outward signs of inward grace? I would like to ask the reverend gentleman a question: Considering the position for influencing public opinion which the clergy occupy, locally and nationally, who or what is to blame for such a sad condition of affairs when "on the Stock Exchange the Jew could discern no difference between the professing Christian and himself in a readiness to take an unfair advantage of another," (a) the cleric, (b) the lay, or (c) the system? Facial contortions in the hope of deceiving ourselves ' on the Sabbath that "heaven is our home" are speedily removed on the Monday when we change our clothes and rapidly assume the business smile. Then, business is business and he who dares to carry his Christianity into work-a-day worldly affairs quickly receives a rude shock, and he forever holds his peace—or loses his place. I hope some kind friend will be graciously pleased to bring the question before the notice of the reverend gentlemon in the hope that a reply may be forthcoming. Robert Louis Stevenson once wrote" Each man should learn what is within him, that he, may strive to mend ; he must be taught what is without him that he may be kind to others."

I would remind all social workers that the Sale of Work in connection with the I.L.P. will be held on Saturday next in the Gymnasium,Palmyra Square and that their presence is requested at the opening, 2.30 afternoon. Mr. T. Russell Williams (Parliamentary Candidate of Huddersfield) is to officiate and Mr. J. A.. Seddon, M.P., will be chairman. There will be first- class concerts and side shows and a right merry time, is anticipated. Don't mistake the day—Saturday next 2.30 afternoon. There is much need of social reform and there you will find earnest individuals prepared to help. You do your share. MR. J. C. P...A.RR'S UN ND CUT

at "those who prefer idleness to work" is likely to be remembered in Warrington for many days, and there will doubtless be a big display of political fire- works when the band plays at the next Parliamentary Election. The voter is rapidly realizing the fallacy of believing all the nice things that are said at Elections and be is taking more notice of speeches delivered in between times.

DEMOS writes :—Mr. J. C. Parr at the Primrose League meeting accused the Government of unduly curtailing the expenditure on our army and navy "to get money for a visionary, scheme of old age pensions; and for subsidising the unemployed; in other words, those who prefer idleness to work, and would rather be fed out of the pockets of others than earn a decent livelihood for themselves." That is a rather" tall" assertion, John, for I discover that at the end of February, in 273 trade unions making returns,23,932 members were reported as unemployed. Does Mr. Parr really believe that all those men and women "prefer idleness to work" and would rather be receiving " out of work " benefit (out of the pockets of their fellow members) than earning a decent livelihood for themselves? Have they not yet heard in Grappenhall of the Leicester unemployed march to London, and is Mr. Parr not aware of the fact that the late Conservative Government passed a so-called Work- man's Unemplved Act and that Right to Work, and Distress Committees have been formed in most of the industrial centres in the United Kingdom? Would the men who have been threatened with discharge ;rom Woolwich rather be fed out of the pc2kets of others than earn a de zent livelihood for themselves? If so, why did they march in procession to the House of Commons and demand an interview with respcnsible Cabinet Ministers? It is cruelly untrue to say that an unemployed man is necessarily " lazy," and if .t r. Parr•will pay a visit to the docks of all our large sea- port towns he will, as a rule, find a number of men willing to work but unable to obtain even a casual job. I would also direct his attention to the fact that the average weekly wage of adult manual labour is under £1 and many thousands of "fully employed" men and women are at all times unable to earn a decent livelihood for themselves. "GAS WORKER"writes as follows:—Millionaires or reputed millionaires hardly care to take an intelligent interest in the welfare of the toiling millions and when any laudable effort is made by the state to improve the conditiort of the masses the well fed encloses (when not seeking Parliamentary honours) make v1g0r(418 kicks at their unfortunats Christian "brothers "at the bottom of the social ladder. This is the class, these stalwarts of society, that lecture the unemployed, tell them they" prefer idleness to work and would rather be fed out of the pockets of others thol earn a livelihood for themselves." The poor devil that is seeking employ ment Icom workshop to workshop, losing hope and courage,and experiencing a misery and a black despair that cannot be measured by money, is the target of their attack.

ANOTHER " INVESTIGATION " COMMITTEE.

Warrington has become notorious during rec3nt years for its numerous Investigation Committees. We might ask here what has b3COIlle. of the Lawson Con- tract Investigation Committe ? Is it dead or only sleer.:ng ? The latest committee of inquiry, however, has been, if rumour is correct, appointed by the House Committae, of the Board of Guardians. One welcomes any sign of a spirit of ecomomy, but surely those responsible for this investigation cannot be guided by a-spirit of tr ae economy on this occasion. The duties of this Committee are to find out whether one of the aged paupers is deEerving of the extravagant luxury of one ounce of tobacco 'per week. . . _

What is the standsra of conduct by which this man is to be judged? It would be interesting to know. How many of us, at seventy years of age, could bear the powerful scarchlight of this Committee, and upon which of u 3 no mark would be found ? Evidently before a , man can enjoy the luxui j of one ounce of tobacco per week in the Warrington Workhode, he must be as free from suspicion as Cwsar's wife. Perhaps the , greatest crime is that be has become poor and charge- able to the rates. Maybe he has been 'guilty of the habit of taking an occasional glass of beer, or of the habit of spotting winners and backing losers. Surely he cannot have indulged in betting on the Stock Exchange or of gambling in human lives, or he might have been in,what is termed," Another Place " where the members wear coronets. However, we can only conjecture what this man's sins may have *been. Perhaps the Committee will enlighten us, when they have concluded their investigations, by making their report public. THE SCOUT.

This is often done by asking the foremen, or masters of the works where they are employed for a "sub," or part payment for the few hours or the day's work which they have done. THE TIES ON THE STUMP.

[Br MR. J. HOWES.] In "the good old days" when the Tories had absolute power, and the Liberalism of our time was a thing entirely unknown, public meetings in the large towns met with very little encouragement. The people were frequently spoken of ai "the great unwashed" and were considered too ignorant to be consulted on National matters. If they attempted to get together a number of their fellow-creaeures for the purpose of considering the extdhaion of the franchise, or the operation of some of the unjust laws which at that time burdened the Statute Book, and made it almost impossible for the working-classes to live, their meet- ings were frequently broken up by the soldiers and others acting under the authority of the Government. Perhaps this reign of terror was the greatest under the Liverpool Administration, which lasted from 1812 to 1827, during which period the Manchester massacre took place, and riots in various parts of the country were of common occurrence. But now a change has come o'er the scene; the battle fought by our fathers, has been won, and the right of public meetings is an accomplished fact. So strong has public opinion become, that even the Tories are compelled to bow before it. Real live Dukes and Earls condescend to address the people in public meeting assembled. The Tory Party generally, are •taking their r3regrinations, begging of the pe )ple they so long despised to send them back to power. It therefore behoves the citizens of this country to he on their guard against sophistry and misrepresentations, specimens of which come to hand every day. The Liberal Government is accused of desiring to confiscate the landlord's- property, to rob the poor man of his beer, to destroy reliii, to abandon our Colonies, to neglect the fleet, and ruin the army. In fact, the Tory Party, claiming a monopoly of all the virtues and posing as the only patriots in the country, would have the people believe that the result of the last General Election, was a great National calamity. All this, despite the fact that the last eight years of Tory Rule, with all its horrors; with all its blunders and failures; with all its expenditure of Treasure and blood, must still be fresh in the memories of the people. The present Liberal Government means business. Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman is in earnest, and although monoply and class privilege are firmly entrenched in this country, if the people,will only watch the develop- ment of political events, and give their friends in office the enthusiastic support they deserve, good work will be accomplished. The Labour Members, a number of whom we have had the privilege of listen- ing to in -Warrington, and with whom the member for this borough is in touch and frequent communication, can be relied upon to see that justice is done to those whose interests have been frequently overlooked. A curious instance of Tory Patriotism was given to the world a few days ago by the 1900 club. This club is a Tory institution. It came into existence to cele- brate the Tory victories of that period. On Thursday, April 18th, this institution entertained the Colonial Premiers in the Albert Hall, London. In sending out the Press invitations the Liberal Papers were excluded. The tickets for admission came from the Central Con- servative organisation. Mr. Balfour presided, and at this meeting a letter was read from Mr. Joseph Chamberlain, and " three cheers" were given for " Joe" A great deal of high falutin was indulged in, and the Tory Jingo patriots seem to have had a high time of it, but the most brilliant idea • which had occurred to the organisers of this meeting, was to spread a huge Union Jack on the floor as a carpet, so that during the delivery of the so-called Patriotic speeches, the audience were able to wipe their feet on our National Flag. We are not told what became of this carpet after the demonstration, but I would snggest that it should be preserved, and exhibited at future Tory D3monstrations. What a howl of indignation would have gone up from the Tory Press, if this degradation had taken place at a Liberal gathering. The late Lord Beaconsfield, once referred to the Tories as the " stupid Party." It seems that they have not improved much since his day, but I think intelligent, and truly patriotic people, who love their country and its flag, have a right to protest against the insult offered to the Union Jack by the Tory Jingoes of the 1900 Club.

The object of the Tory Party to-day is of course to overthrow the present Government,because they know that the Liberal Party are pledged to the introduction of reforms which, by extending the power of the people, will strike another blow at class privilege, and consequently limit the power of Toryism for all time ; already our opponents are furious, because Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman, has been suggesting Land Law Reform. He thinks the land should be used by the people, and he finds that in a great many parts of the country, those who control these matters absolutely I refuse to grant the use of land for cultivation, or the erection of labourers' cottages. The toilers may work and pay taxes, but the land, and the game and other territorial privileges, must be m mopolised by those people, who, Mr. Chamberlain once told us, "Toil not, neither, do they spin." This division as proposed by the Tory Party, reminds me of the gentleman and his black servant who on one occasion went out on a day's shooting, the result of a hard day's work being the fall of two birds one a pheasant, and the other a black crow. When the work was over, the master said to his servant, "Samba, we have only killed t% o birth, but we Will divide the spoil equally. I'll take the pheasant, and you can have the crow,or you can take the crow, and I'll have the pheasant." Sambo seemed perplexed, and after thinking a moment, said ." Master, s 7 that again." "Well," said the gentleman, "I was just observing, that I would take the Pheasant, and you could have the crow,or that you could take the crow, and I would have the pheasant," " Yaas," said Sambo " Dat seems bery fair, but somehow, or udder, de black crow allus comes to me." And so the people will find that, if they allow the Tories to divide the good things, the crows will go to the Poor, and the pheasants to the Rich.

The Liberals have given the people the vote, thereby providing them with the power of defending them- selves against their political enemies. Now we must demand a Radizal alteration of our Land Laws, so that the men who are willing to cultivate may do so, with profit to themselves, and advantage to the nation. A great many other Reforms are also needed. We must not rest until we have succeeded in making this grand old country of ours, a land where the people are respect3d, not on account of the length of their purses, but because of the power of their intellect and the work of their hands. The Worker is .of more impol tance than the Idler, and those who are willing to labour must have an opportunity of doing So under the most favourable conditions.

PAL FRY SALARY.

Much surprise was expressed at the last meeting of the Richmond Corporation Free Library Committee when it became known that one of the assistants, who is 22 years of age, and has been in the employ of the committee six years, was only receiving us. a week. The committee increased his salary to 15s. a week. At the same meeting the salary of the porter was increased to 255. a week.

CUTICURA REMEDIES ENTIRELY CURED HIM

"I had been troubled with swellings under the armpits and on the small of the back accompanied by a burning itch to such an extent that I got very little rest, day or night. This is the season of London A SOCIAL picture shows. The Royal REVOLUTION. Institute Galleries, the New Gallery, the Gallery of the 'Society of British Artists—these are open already, and the Royal Academy will open on Monday. People crowd into these exhibitions much as of old, but not, as we are given to understand, for the purpose of buying pictures. They are there as spectators, students of the art movement of the time, or just ordinary uncul- tured folk, to whom picture exhibitions supply a mode .of passing the time. But they are not there as buyers, it seems. Nobody buys pictures nowadays; hardly anybody, at least. The artists are taking to teaching, or if they are not painters of portraits, they get into the hands of the money-lenders, and then facile is their descent into the region of continuous penury. But why has the public ceased to buy pictures, except, perhaps, such little inexpensive things as may be had for anything up to five or ten pounds? The main reason, -we surmise, is the coming of the motor-car, which is revolu- tionising our civilisation. Those who never kept carriages keep motor-cars. A motor-car is thus an additional expense. It cuts something 'away from the margin which permitted of indulgence in luxuries. But it does a great deal more than that: it enables its owner to live out lof town without dependence on trains. He gives up his town residence and sets up "a little place in the country." He lives at his club in town, ' end gets to his little place to sleep at night. What does he want with pictures ?: He has no time to enjoy them; he has really no place to put them in. A man in blinkers and a deerskin coat is out of harmony with pictures. He sends such as were contained in his town house to Christie's, and buys no more. And this is not all the harm that he has done by setting up a motor-car. The owner of his house in town was in all likelihood a buyer of pictures, but he ict having his houses thrown on his hands a dozen at a time by the owners of motor-cars. How can he any longer buy pictures ? The coining of the motor has been accompanied by the loss of a great deal of what may be called house pride, so far as the art of the painter is con- cerned, at least. Men would drag their friends home in order to show their pictures to them. An indispensable feature of a large house was its picture gallery. But these galleries are full. That is one reason why artists are now, as a rule, railing at the spitefulness of fortune. Another is, as we have indicated, the decay of house pride; but the most powerful of all, it is probable, is a house pride of a new and feminine character. The lady of the house calls your attention to the beauty of her walls, a plain, fiat drab, perhaps, or a peacock blue, or a delicate shade of green. lf there were any pictures, they have been sent upstairs to the garrets, and a small cheap thing has been got in because it has a plack frame. It is placed in a corner, like the signature on a Japanese fan, and makes one dusky, out- standing spot on a background of green or grey or reddish wall paper ; and so it is that we are doing without the artist, though we visit the art exhibi- tions. The problem is, should this condition of things continue how are the exhibitions to be carried on?

The tragic suddenness of THE LATE MR. L. the death of Mr. Lewis W. REYNOLDS. Reynolds came as a surprise and a shock to many people. Though not a prominent public man he was very well-known and a farailar figure in the town. Thera are hundreds of people who knew Mr. Reynolds by sight and who have a vivid recollection of the grey-haired, wiry, and active old gentleman who rarely wore an overcoat in the coldest weather but always carried a hand bag which seemed an inseparable companion. The why and wherefore of that bag were a mystery to the curious. It was rumoured, however, that the deceased gentleman frequently indulged in little kindnesses to the poor children and that the contents of the bag were necessary to his charitable little scheme. These and other personal characteristics made Mr. Reynolds a noticeable man in the daily walks of life, but he was most intimately known to the members of the Musical Society for whom ke rendered ungrudging service over a period of forty years. At the time of his death he was hon. treasurer, and his place will be difficult to adequately fill.

The work which is carried RAGGED &Rom. on by the Warrington WORK. Ragged School amongst the poverty-stricken children of the slum parts of the town must often be depressing. In face of many obstacles and with comparatively little encouragement from their fellow townspeople,the workers have pursued their mission faithfully for twenty years, and their , reward has been to know that they have been the means of shedding sunshine in many a darkened home and of counteracting the evil influences which often mar young lives. Councillor Skelland, the honoured founder and superintendent of the institution, has never allowed his zeal to flag and , he has been ably seconded by Mr. Owen. It was I somewhat surprising to hear at the anniversary meeting on Monday that these ardent workers have travelled 20,000 miles by rail and over 4,000 miles on foot with the drum and fife band in order to raise the wherewithal to clothe and feed children under their care. The institution has a record of useful service to its credit,

and a forward movement on social lines is to be embarked upon. Full details were not announced, but we gather that premises have been acquired with the object of opening a labour home. This, it is hoped, will fit the children to take a part in industrial life and will also lend help to deserving unemployed who desire a night's shelter. Funds are urgently required to carry out the project and we trust that when an appeal is made the response will be generous. The anniversary celebrations were unique from the fact that. the workers were honoured with a visit from Mr. John Kirk, Secretary of the Rigged School Union. His addresses were of that high tone which evidences a true solicitude on behalf of the little ones and his cheering words of encouragement will not soon be forgotten.

When the Rev R. J. Camp THE CHURCH bell takes away the breath COUGH. of his congregation at the City Temple by expounding his views on his "New Theology," he objects to their indulgence in coughing. But he is not the first prelate who has quarrelled with a man for coughing. In " Othello Shakespeare writes : "Shut the door; cough, or cry ' hem,' if acybody come." Precisely ; the church cough is first cousin to the hem, and to utter hems is to make a stammering noise. But there is the secular as well as the churchy hem, the former being familiar amongst gossips and causing frequent tittering or laughter. The parsonic cough, us depicted by the nervous curate on the stage, when he places his gloved right hand ic front of his mouth, is a discreet blending together of a' variety of mixed emotions. But the church cough must not be confounded with that bearing the name of the "churchyard '' ; for the former is as a gentle zephyr, whilst the latter resemblqs a cataclysm, so that, when a lodger on the fourth-floor-back has a "churchyarder" it. distraises the worthy landlady in the basement kitchen. In " Love's Labour Lost," the words "And coughing drowns the parson's saw " show us that preachers have been afflicted by such interruptions for centuries. How, therefore, can Mr. Campbell expect to eliminate these noises ? Coughing arises from many different causes. In church, it is no doubt most often due to irritation of the throat by the inhalation of damp air, or dust raised by sweeping the matting just before ser- vice. It is frequently noticed that, in a service for men only, there is far less coughing than where the congregation consists chiefly of ladies, who, in summer-time, collect in their trains on the way to the place of worship a hoard of irritating particles of road-sweepings, and when one member of an assembly begins expelling the air from the lungs others sympathetically follow suit. To cure the trouble it may be necessary to pass round a new- theology voice lozenge.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The cracked cemetery bell is to be replaced with a new one. At least, so we are tolled !

A morning paper tells us that the King's dinner to the Colonial Premiers will be a dinner to gentlemen only, "but . . . will include a number of promi- nent polisicians." _ *

All future Warrington cases of cerebro-spinal meningitis (spotted fever) are to be treated at the Sanatorium at Sankey. The inhabitants were nervous before, but they will be more so in future, unless familiarity has bred contempt for contagion ere this. * * _

A follower of the old 'ways writes from Matlock Bath to complain that "The Lovers' Walk has been closed to the public, and its name changed to Corpora- tion Pathway." Adding insult to injury. Municipal authorities are sadly lacking in romance. Gee

The Sanitary Works Committee is doing a rare bnsiness in that useful but mal-odorous commodity, manure, and at present the demand exceeds .the supply. We wonder if this branch of municipal trading will eventually contribute something in relief of the rates.

The new theatre has received the blessing of the Rector and of the Rev. Father Bryne. The latter . used very complimentary language at the luncheon and said " if a syndicate of angels came down to one of the entertainments they would not do anything but applaud with their wing:." A light-hearted Frenchman was patiently watching a four hours' exhibition of the anchor stroke the other day, and at last his face brightened. " What colour would you call the particular anchor ?" he asked his friend, and the latter, promptly giving it up, the cheerful son of G-a'ul replied : ." Well, in my own language, I should say c'est Reece." Its injuries are not so serious as might have been expected. "London Opinion."

* * Penketh has at last got a Justice of the Peace, and we congratulate Dr. Murray and the village on the honour jointly conferred upon them. At the first meeting of the newly-elected Rural District Council on Wednesday, Dr. Murray was appointed chairman with great unanimity. The office carries with it a seat on the magisterial bench, where the genial doctor should be an acquisition. In Penketh he doctors all the natives, and heads the Parish Council poll with ease. It seems quite fitting that he should be both medicine- man and law-giver. * *

The Liverpool diocese has an Archdeacon Spooner, but apparently relies on Canon Sylvester for its Spoonerisms. At a vestry at Roby Church the rev. gentleman had the misfortune to refer to Church- warden Appleyard as Alr.Churchyard Applewarden.At the same meeting, Alderman William Roberts not to be outdone made a blunder of another sort. In an impassioned speech in favour of the Church Discipline Bill, he declared that English Protestants would be satisfied so long as clergymen conformed with Thirty- Nine Articles of Asssociation.

With the view of ridiculing "The Mikado" incident, Mr. Vincent Kennedy has given notice that he will ask the Prime Minister whether his attention had been called to the fact. that in the play of " Hamlet " the King of Denmark, js portrayed as a murderer; and whether, in view of the fact that Denmark is a friendly Power, and this reference to the King is likely to cause offence in Denmark, he will ask the Lord Chamberlain to prohibit the production of this play ; and whether he intends to bring in legisla- tion to define and limit the powers of the Lord Chamberlain.

The laying of the foundation stone of the new Palace Theatre and Hippodrome on Wednesday passed off with great eclat. There was a large and repre- sentative assembly, the weather was kind for once, the speech-making was most interesting, and everything augured well for the success of the venture. To Mr. H. E. Barnes, upon whom the arrangements of the function had devolved, this happy consummation of his efforts must have been particularly gratifying. The building promises to be a distinct acquisition_, and there is every indication that the enterprise will be well supported by the townspeople. _*_* *

More will be heard of the suspension of the licence for "The Mikado." The whole affair (says the London correspondent of the "Liverpool Post and Mercury ") is involved in mystery, and no one has yet been able to gather why the Lord Chamberlain, Lord Althorp, took action in the matter. It was at first assumed that some representations had been made by the Japanese Government on the matter, but this does not appear to have been the case. The latest story is that the Lord Chamberlain was acting under sug- gestions from the very highest quarter in this country. This rumour may be as unfounded as others, but the solid fact remains that the public performance of "The Mikado," has been prohibited in this country. Apart from anything else, this is a serious loss to the owner of the copyright, who appears to be Mrs. D'Oyly Carte. Mr. Gilbert has been interviewed on i the subject, and he s quite as unable as anyone else to account for the action of the authorities. The question is likely, I hear, to engage the attention of the society which is concerned with the rights of dramatic authors.

* * * , Quite a bombshell descended upon the London and orth-Western staff at the Arpley offices this week 'when it was learned that the popular district manager, Mr. I. T. Williams, had been promoted to the London office and would cease work at Warrington immedi- ately. Mr. Williams has only been in Warrington about four years but in that time he has done so 3zuch for the employees of the company that they hold him in the very highest regard. He was more- over a most energetic manager, and possessed of a Personal charm which made him popular outside, as well as inside, official circles. The platitude that e.` Warrington's loss is London's gain" bears on it the 'rapress of truth. Everyone will wish Mr. Williams Well in his new sphere, and we are sure that he will 12Irver forget the town whose welfare he considered so v'asely during the time he was numbered among its People. ;

- * * The congregation of Bold-street Church are to be oogratulated upon the magnificent success of their °azaar in the Parr Hall. Those who have not already visited the bazaar should make a point of doing so to-day, when the children's opening cere- znony —one of the prettiest little functions of its kind 'We have ever seen—will be repeated.

* * We are pleased to learn that a volume of poems by eY the late Mr. Luke. Hamilton Talbot,Chief Constable of Warrington, is shortly to be published. Long before kr. Talbot won such universal fame as a brilliant Young detective he had been a prolific writer, and it is felt by his friends that many of his poems in particular are worthy of a permanent place in English literature, and that no better monument to his memory could be devised than a volume of selections. The book will ontain one or more portraits of the author, and will Include a sketch of his adventurous career and his exploits as a policeman by his lifelong friend, Dr. Pharles F. Forshaw, of Bradford,editor of" Yorkshire Notes and Queries" and of several well-known „aathologies,and author of "Masonic Poems," &c. aß_.ether with some details of his life and work on arrington and an appreciation of his writings by 4311.:, Arthur Bennett, editor of "The Dawn" and author Sunrise Songs'" &c. The profits arising from sale of the book,which will be published at 3s. 6d., Will be handed to Mr. Talbot's widow.
