LADIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER THE CLOCK, BRIDGE-STREET.—Adtv. The Late Rev. S. Wilkinson.

The funeral of the late Rev. S. Wilkinson whose death we recorded last week took place at the Timperley Parish Church on Saturday amid many manifestations of regret. A. sh mt service was held at Halton con- ducted by the Rev. Charles Wray,assisted by the Rev. H. B. Blogg, rural dean, and the Rev. C. H. Kirby Turner. Among those present were Sir Richard and Lady Brooke, the Rev. C. H. Killick, the Rev. A. P. Reynolds (vicar of Kingsley), Rev. W. Bracecamp (vicar of St. Paul's, Warrington). The remains were conveyed to Timperley by road. The cortege was preceded by the Timperley Company of the Church Lads' Brigade, who walked with inverted arms. Amongst the mourners present were: —The Rev. H. B. Firth, St. James', Latchford; the Rev. H. V. Pigot, rector of Grappenhall •, the Rev. T. S. Toft, formerly of Stockton Heath. Amongst the floral tributes was a wreath foom the St. Paul's Mothers' Meeting. —Fitting references to the deceased's work in Warrington were made on Sunday at St. Paul's by the Rev. W. Bracecamp, and at St. Barnabas by the Rev. J. H. G. Hollingworth.

Death of Mr. Philip Darbyshire. A. prominent Cheshire farmer passed away last week in the person of Mr. Philip Darbyshire, who for 41 years had occupied Newton Bank Farm, Daresbury. The deceased was well known in the neighbourhood, having held the offices of Churchwarden and assistant overseer for 45 years. The funeral took place at the Daresbury Parish Church on Monday, the Rev. T. T. Lee Jones conducting the service. The chief mournerers were: Dr. Bower, Messrs. New Organ at Emmanuel Church The Organ which until recently was in Bold-street Wesleyan Church has been completly, overhauled and rebuilt in Emmanuel Church and the opening

service will be held on Wednesday evening next: Mr. W. Hickman will deliver addresses. Wear BANK'S Ladies and Gent's Gloves.—Advt. Military Funeral. The funeral of the late Sergeant Rodd, of the King's Liverpool Regiment, Orford Barracks, took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Monday, and was attended with full military honours. The deceased was very popular in the Barracks, and about 150 of • the men were present at the interment. The band of the depot and a firing party (eighteen strong) also attended and fired volleys over the grave. The pro- ; ceedings were of an impressive character, and were witnessed by many townspeople.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. Election of Chairman.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural Dis- trict Council was held at Bewsey Chambers on Wednes- day afternoon. The Clerk (Mr. J. C. Sutton) presided at the commencement, and there were also present : Dr. Murray, Messrs. A. Brooke, W. Houghton, G. Mather, W. Furness, P. Longton, J. Backhouse, W. Stanier, R. Forshaw, J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (deputy clerk), B. S. Newcombe (surveyor), H. H. &image (deputy surveyor), Dr. Scphton (medical officer), and F. W. Kerfoot (sanitary inspector).

===== 0002601_19070504_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTION OF CHAIRMAN.

Mr. BROOKS proposed the election of Dr. Murray as chairman for the ensuing year. He said the doctor would make an ideal chairman. Mr. MATHER seconded and the resolution was passed unanimously. Dr. MURRAY, on taking the chair, thanked the Coun- cil for the honour they had conferred upon him. He hoped with assistance of the members during his year of office, that the business of the Council would be conducted in such a manner that the chair would not be lowered in its character. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19070504_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
DEPUTY CHAIRMAN.

Mr. WILLIAM HOLIGHToN remarked that their County Councillor(Mr.R.Forshaw)was the senior member of the Council who had not passed the chair. He therefore proposed. that he be elected vice-chairman with the view at some future time to succeeding to the chair. Mr. MATHER seconded and the resolution was carried. Mr. FORSHA.w briefly thanked the Council. COMMITTEES AND OFFICERS. It was decided that the Roads Committee consist of the whole Council.

On the motion of Mr. MATHER, seconded by the CHAIRMAV, it was resolved that Dr. Richard, Sephton be re-appointed Medical Officer of Health for the year ending June 24th, 1908, at the salary for such year of .£lso,and that Mr. Frank Witter Kerfoot be re-appointed Inspector of Nuisances for the year ending June 24th, 1908, at the salary of £l5O, with an allowance for the cost of the customary uniform.

THE DUST NUISANCE. A letter was read from the Parish Council of Rixtcn- with-Glazebrook bringing before the notice of the District Council the nuisance caused to the inhabitants by the clouds of dust on the main roads, and they also suggested that the channels along the main roads were not in a very satisfactory condition. They suggested the sweeping of the dust, &c., from the channels and asked that the surveyor's early attention be given to it. Mr. BrtooKs said he had had a conversation with the Surveyor, and he thought that the matter would be satisfactorily dealt with. Mr. HOUGHTON : It will be,a record if this motor dust can be satisfactorily dealt with. A further letter was read from the Winwick-with- Hulme Parish Council complaining of the dust nuisance from motor traffic. They asked the District Council to do something to stop the nuisance, which was causing severe damage to farmers' crops. They thought the very great speed was a cause of so much dust and sug- gested a reduction in speed of motors. The CLERK remarked that the speed of motors was really a matter for the police. The CHAIRMAN said that the Council wrote to the police about twelve months ago, but there seemed to be little difference.

Mr. BACKHOUSE suggested that a salt solution be placed on one of the roads. The SURVEYOR said a salt solution caused the roads to be wet much longer than otherwise. It had a damp effect.

Mr. FURNESS : I suggest that the best place totttry it is on Liverpool-road. (Laughter.) Mr. BROOKS: I agree. It would be all right on Liverpool high road, in Rixton. (Laughter.) The SURVEYOR suggested a number of small water carts about the district.

Mr. BROOKS: The effect of water carts does not last ten minutes on a summer's day. The SURVEYOR replied that if the water were put on at night, it would have time to harden and to cake the road.

A letter also complaining of the dust nuisance was read from the Poulton-with-Fearnhead Parish Council, and the whole matter was referred to the Roads Committee

SPUrTLE BRIDGE.

Letters were read from the Parish Councils of Poulton-with-Fearnhead and Woolston-with-Martins- croft, suggesting that as Spittle Bridge had to be rebuilt it would be much better to take the road behind the cottages than to spend money on simply rebuilding the present bridge. The CLERK said the District Council were supporting the larger scheme of taking the road behind the cottages.

HEAVY MOTOR TRAFFIC. Alluding to a letter from the Poulton-with-Fearnhead Parish Council which complained of the heavy motor traffic which went through the district, Mr. MATHER said he was glad that one of the firms which had caused much annoyance to the district had ceased to send out their vans.

Mr. HOUGHTON said that the worst offender of all still came through the district. He had discovered that the County Council had power to limit the weights which should be taken by hettvy motors, and the Cheshire County Council exercised that power, with the result that one firm which was not allowed to go throuh Cheshire owing to the enormous weights it carried came regularly along Manchester-road. He moved that the Lancashire County Council's attention be called to this matter, and that they place a limit on the weight allowed to be carried along the roads. Mr. BACKHOUSE seconded and the resolution was carried.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
WARBURTON BRIDGE COMMITTEE.

Messrs. Brooks and Houghton were re-appointed as representatives of the Council on the above committee

LIGHTING OF IVINWICR ROAD.

A letter from the Clerk to the Winwick-with-Hulme Parish Council requested the Council to allow a repre- sentative from the Parish Council on the above com- mittee, but the request was not entertained, and Messrs. Longton, Ball, Brooks and Stanier were appointed as the committee.

MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT. I Dr. SEPHTON reported that during the past month there had been 23 births and 24 deaths. The death rate was equal to 235 per 1,000 against 23.0 the average of the last three months,and 27•0 the corresponding month of last year. There had been 17 deaths reported in the County Asylum at Winwick, and the death rate proper for the district was 613 per 1,000. There had been one death from cerebro-spinal meningitis (spotted fever). During the month the following cases of infectious disease were reported :—Scarlet fever, 3 ; diphtheria, 3. The report was adopted. _ PURCHASE OF WATER CARTS.

On the recommendation of the SURVEYOR, it was decided to purchase two water carts at £l4 10s. each.

ner, Saturday, May 4, 1907.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Lively Scene.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday under the presi- dency of Mr. W. Houghton. There were also present the Rev. T. R. Matthews (vice-chairman), Mrs. Wilkinson, Mrs. Simpson, Miss Broadbent, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, J. Billinge, W. Gill, R. Bolton, A. Brooks, P. Longton, G. Mather, W. Furness, W. C. Brooks, W• Flannagan, T. Ritchie, E, L. Bent, J. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright (superintendent of Padgate Schools).

A BREEZE. When the minutes of the last meeeing were read, Mr. JOLLEY pointed out that there was no record of a reso- lution appointing the Chairman, the Clerk, and himself as a committee to superintend the preparation of the year book. The CLERK said he had not considered the matter sufficiently important to place it on the minutes. The CHAIRM AN said he did not consider a formal resolution necessary. It was simply a casual instruction. Mr. JOLLEY said his desire was to prevent inaccuracies which had occurred in previous year books. The CLERK challenged Mr. Jolley to prove his state- I ment-

Mr. JOLLEY said there were at least thirty errors in the current year book. The CLERK : I shall tender my resignation this minute if that statement is allowed to go uncontra- dicted.

PADGATE SCHOOLS.

DEATH OF THE ROMAN CATIIOLIC INSTRUCTOR.

At a meeting of the Pacigate Schools Committee Mr. Mather was re-elected chairman and Mr. Bent was appointed vice-chairman. The death of Father Butler, the Roman Catholic instructor,was reported with regret and it was resolved that the superintendent be empowered to arrange for a temporary successor and to remunerate him at the same rate as hitherto. Miss Ivey's resignation was tendered and accepted with regret and the superintendent was directed to advertise for a successor to live out, at a salary of £45 if uncertilicated and £65 if certificated. Mr. MATHER said Miss ivey had been a very success- ful teacher and would be greatly missed.

The minutes were appro-ved. HOUSE COMMITTEE. ALTERATIONS IN TIIE LUNACY WARD.

At a meeting of the House Committee Mr. Billinge was elected chairman and Mr. Mather vice-chairman. The Ladies' Visiting Committee was appointed as follows :—Mrs. Fairclough, Mrs. Rowson, Mrs. Pierpoint, Mrs. J. L. Whittle, Mrs. Collingwood, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Mrs. Frost, Mrs. J. E. Wright, Mrs. Liston and Mrs. Simpson. The Brabazon Ladies were re-elected with the addition of Mrs. Furness. The Clerk was directed to prepare a report on the Workmen's Compensation Act as to the liability of the Guardians to persons in their employ, and to obtain estimates for the insurance by the Guardians of all persons to whom they would be legally liable for compensation. Owing to an outbreak of measles and chicken-px it was decided to appoint a temporary night nurse at a salary of 253. and travelling expenses. The tender of Messrs. W.and A.Ashton amounting to 56 7s. 9d.for alterations to the lunacy ward was accepted. Mr. BILLINGE reported that the temporary night engineer had resigned, and a sub-committee recom- mended the appointment for two months of Mr. Sydney Roberts, Bold-street. With regard to the alterations in the lunacy ward they were bound to carry out the recommendations of the Commissioners. With reference to the Workmen's Compensation Act he was delighted to think that during the past seventy years none of the servants of the Board had met with serious accident.

The CLERK referred at some length to the Act, and said complete information as to the servants to whom the Board would be liable in case of accident would be prepared. All persons were included except the Clerk —(laughter)---and if he unfortunately met with an acci- dent he would not come within the Act. The question wanted very serious consideration, and he suggested the appointment of a practical committee with Mr. Billinge, who had had considerable experience in insurance matters, as chairman. The minutes were approved. DISTRESS COMMITTEE.

The Board's representatives on the Distress Com- mittee were re-elected with the substitution of Mrs. Simpson and Mr. Gill for Mrs. Clay and Mr. Payton.

EXPENDITURA-TIME TO HALT.

The CLERK said he had received a letter from the Clerk to the County Council stating that the amount required for the county expenditure during the coming year %mild be practically the same as for last year. It would not be possible to arrive at the exact amount of the rate until the balances could be ascertained for the year ended 31st March. It was not expected, however, that there would be any increase in the rates except in the case of those townships on which there would be levied a special Education rate in respect of the pro- vision.of Council schools. The CHAIRMAN : It 1:7-ould be MOTO Comforting if they could indicate a slight decrease. Mr. EILLINGE said there had been an agitation in the country;with reference to the expenditure. The Guardians too were not exempt from the all-searching eye of the nation on Poor Law Administration and in that Union they had attained a very high standard of spending. Now that the County Council had raised the question the Guardians might say that they too were just about to halt. The nation was at present asking whether Poor Law Administration did pay and the auditing of the accounts was more rigid than ever before. The agitation at Westminster and the attitude of Mr. John Burns on the question of how the destitute should be relieved and whether the present system was the cheapest, most profitable and best for the unfortu- nate, was largely before the country. He wanted to emphasise the fact that the time had come in that Union when they must halt. They had incurred expen- diture in equipping their establishments and making them all that could be desired and now was the time for rigid administration.

Mr. J. M. McGrath, J.P.

, Mr. J. M. McGrath has just been elected chairman of the Hendon District Council. He was born 57 years ago in Ireland, but his early life was spent in Warrington where his father was storekeeper for the railway contractors, Brassey and Goodfellow. He was • educated at St. Alban's school, Warringtori. Mr. McGrath now holds an important position under Messrs. W. and T. Avery, the eminent engineers and weighing-machine manufacturers.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

PRIMITIVE METHODIST ANNIVERSARY.

The 42nd anniversary of the Primitive Methodist Church, Legb-street, Earlestown, was celebrated on Sunday. The preacher was the Rev. W. Carr, of Heck- mondwike. Special hymns were sung by the children and solos rendered by the Misses May Parrott and Winnie Williams. Anthems were also contributed in good style by the choir. In the afternoon a sacred cantata, "Jerusalem," was given, the various singers , doing exceedingly well in the solos and quartettes. ' The soloists were: Miss Woods (soprano),Miss Stronach (contralto), Mr. Parsons (tenor), Mr. G. Edmonds (bass); quartettes were given by the Misses M. Stronach and E. Leyland and Mr. J. Sproston and Mr. E. Mullins. The choir under the leadership of Mr. G. Edmonds: did excellent service. Miss Jackson presided at the piano, and Mr. R. Robinson at the organ. The collections amounted to £5O 10s. 7.41.

DEATH OF MR. SAMUEL TVVISS.

A sensation was caused over the week-end by the tragic death of Mr. Samuel Twiss, estate foreman for Lord Newton, who lived in High-street, Newton-le-Willows, and whose body was on Frida,3 recovered from a pond in Golborne Park. It appears that Mr. Twiss who was about 35 years of age had been missing from the previous day. The deceased's bicycle was found in an omhard in the park on Friday afternoon, and this circumstance resulted in an examination being made of Newton Lake. The body was discovered in a pond some distance away, and was removed home by the police to await the inquest. The deceased. who was a single man and lived with his mother, who is a widow, was mcch respected by the tenantry on his lordship's estate.—The inquest on the body was held on Mond ly afternoon at Newton-In- Willows Police Court. The evidence showed that the deceased might haveaccideutally slipped into the water and, owing to the state of the slope, that he might have been unable to gain the bank. A verdict of" Accident- ally drowned" was returned, and the jury passed a vote of Condolence with the widowed mother and family.

ST. HELENS.

FOUND DEAD IN BED.

William Clarke, a hawker, belonging to Lancaster, travelled to St. Helens on Saturday to visit his sister at Barr-street, St. Helens. and on Sunday morning he was there found dead in bed. Clarke was fifty.one years of age, and had been in a bad state of health.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
HUGE ARREARS AT ST. HELENS.

Michael Kennedy, moulder, who Irid been arrested at Manchester, was sent to gaol by the St. Helens magis- trates on Tuesday for three months' hard labour. Kennedy deserted his wife and three children on January 29, 1903, and the guardians had expended £194 12s. 6n. on their maintenance. Kennedy was in receipt of good wages, but, as the Court pointed out, he could not hope to pay off the debt in the ordinary way, so he was sent to gaol. DEATH OF MR. CHARLES CULSHAW.

Mr. Charles CulshavV, who for a long number of years was editor and proprietor of the ` ~` St. Helens Reporter," died on Monday night at the St. Helens Hospital. Mr. Culshaw was about fifty-five years of age. He had all Ids life been connected with the "Reporter," which ten years ago was formed into a company. He was most highly respected by those who knew him intimately. Recently he had been in grievous trouble, and less than a fortnight ago buried his wife, who had been an invalid for many years. On Friday last week he was admitted to the hospital suffering from mien- monia, and his streagth had been undermined to such an extent that he was unable to withstand the ravages of the disease. Mr. Cuishaw leaves one daughter, and his demise will be sincerely regretted by a wide circle of friends.

SUICIDE IN PRESCOT CHURCHYARD. A shocking case of suicide was discovered in the Prescot Parish Churchyard shortly before noon on Wednesday, when the body of an elderly man named Thomas Heffron, a collier, of Highfield-place, Prescot, was found lying under a tree. The man had a scarf fastened round his neck, and his leather waistbelt was hung over a branch of the tree, about eight feet from the ground, the belt having broken, and the body was on the ground. Deceased left home the previous day to go to his work, but had not been seen again until the. body was discovered.

ALLEGED PICKPOCKET AT ST. HELENS.

Florence Morrison, who was said to belong to Lancaster, was remanded at St. Helens on Monday on a charge of stealing a parse and 13s. .71d. from Mrs. Elizabeth Ann Tierney. Mrs. Tierney was in the Market-place on Saturday night about nine o'clock making a purchase at a butcher's stall, when she felt a hand in her pocket. She looked round and saw prisoner withdraw her hand from her pocket. Mrs. Tierney told a man who was near, and he followed Morrison and found the bulk of the money is her hand. She dropped the purse on the floor. Market Inspector Peters was called and took the wonran into custody, recovering 12s. 741.—The Chief Constable said Morrison had made varying statements. She said she came from Barnard Castle, and then Wigan and Lancaster, and it was desirable that she should be remanded for inquiries until next Saturday.

SHOOTING OUTRAGE IN IRELAND.

A daring shooting outrage is reported from the Corofin District, co. Clare. About ten o'clock on Thee- day night a farmer named Murphy, whilst riding homp from a friend's house, was fired at, the shot striking him on the left side of his head and on his shoulder. He was removed to the Corofin Hospital. Murphy, it is stated, had taken part of a local farm regarding which, there is an agitation for partition.

.

A YOUTHFUL POET.

[TO THE EDITOR OF THE " EX&MINER.I

Sir,—The enclosed original verses were written at in request by a lad, ten years of age, attending this school. They are entirely his own composition. The confident assurance of the last line is very interesting, as express- ing the convictions of a Little Englander.—Yours -truly, ALFRED CORNEY. Headmaster, Wycliffe British School. THE WAR MEMORIAL This poem which I try to make, Is, Mr. Corney, for your sake, About the statue glorious. Which shows how brave O'Leary fell, At Pieter's Hill he died so well, When they came out victorious.

Tn the Queen's Gardens. Palmyra.-square, It stands in all its glory there, Reminding us for ever, How brave O'Leary met his doom, And then was laid in his sad tomb, By up forgotten never. When Buller came that festive day, The town was decked with colours gay; Buller was in a carriage, 4 And slowly then he drove along The streets. amid a joyous throng, 'T was gayer than a marriage.

The monument was robed in white, My goodness ! 't was a funny sight ! The 'Mayoress with the Mayor, And many other people too, Watched Buller as away he drew The cloth, as we all stood there.

When it came off we cheered of course, In fact, we shouted ourselves hoarse, And Buller looked quite pleased. But then Sir Redvers drove away, T 13,: people thronging round his way, The cheering never ceased. The monument was in array, Bedecked with wreaths and blossoms gay, On Ladysmith's relief. While heroes brave our cause defend, Old England's glory cannot end, It won't, that's my belief.

S. JEFFERY.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
Modern Business Methods. Rev. W. Bracecamp's Strong Criticism

The Rev. W. Bracecamp, preaching at St. Paul's Church on Sunday, offered a vigorous criticism of modern business methods. One of the greatest scandals of the times was the little regard paid to genuine honesty by business men. If only it would secure a point, or if some pecuniary advantage might be reaped, no scruple was entertained about the course to be taken, and as to whether it be strictly honest or not was reckoned a very secondary consideration. On the Stock Exchange the Jew could discern no differ- ence between the professing Christian and himself in a readiness to take an unfair advantage of another. In the shop how common it was to catl things by wrong names to put a price label on a superior article, whilst an inferior one was given to the customer, or to hide blemishes or defects in that which was sold. With respect to debts how much fraud was practised ! Mon knowingly lived beyond their means, with the intention when necessary of claiming the benefits of the bank- ruptcy court. Property wag secreted, or a legal deed was obtained assigning over to one creditor what ought to be equally divided amongst all. In such cases men often defeated their own purpose, and a straightforward open course would usqally be found to answer far better. The same unfaithfulness was found too often in the officials of joint stock companies, in those who exercised control over the property of others—in clerks, servants, and others. Let them then, whatever their stations in'life might be,manifest a different spirit. They must consider not merely what was law, but what was right. Much that was legallyright might be morally wrong and many things that no human court could ever touch would be deemed heinous offences in the Court of Heaven.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
GREAT SANKEY.

Meeting of the Parish GollllCii. ---•--

A meeting of the Sankey Parish Council was 11,.'cl in the Council school on Monday evening when there were present :—Messrs. a Thompson, W. Taylor, H. Fair- clough, W. Furness, J. W. Walters, and J. Smith (clerk).

===== 0002601_19070504_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. THOMPSON'S RETIREMENT.

Mr. Thompson announced his intention to retire from the Council. It was with great regret that he had to leave them, but his removal was a question of health, and it was impossible for him to retain the position of chairman of the Council any longer. He had endeavoured to do his best for the parish, and be thought he had been fairly successful and if they appreciated the work he had done, be was amply repaid. Mr. Fairclough proposed and Mr. Furness seconded the election of Mr. William Taylor, as chairman for the ensuing year. Mr. Thompson supported and said that all felt that in Mr. Tailor they had a gentleman who was willing to do the beW he could to further the interest of the ratepayers. _ .

Tile-motion was carried unanimously. On the motion of Mr. Taylor, seconded by Mr. Furness, Mr. Fairclongh was appointed vice-chairman.

AN AWFUL FEELING!

Haunted By Strange Fears ; Weak Nervous and Tortured with Indigestion.

LONG-SUFFERING GIRL CURED BY MOTHER SEIGEL'S SYRUP.

Have you ever experienced that haunting dread of something awful about to happen, of a terrible,unknow- able calamity that is to crush you? That is the feeling that weighed on Miss Martha Bannister, of 4, Arden Cottages, West Malvern, and made her life a misery. It comes of shattered nerves and irregular action of the heart, due to indigestion. In a statement dated January Bth, 1907, Miss Bannister says : " I list all appetite, and soon found myself so weak and run down that I could not work. I had constant headaches, often with dizziness, and I was tortured with wind and stomach pains. My nerves got so weak that I could hardly keep still. I cannot describe how I felt sometimes ; it was as if awful things were going to happen, and I usod to throw myself on the couch crying helplessly, Oh, Mother, Mother !' Often I went into a half-fainting state, hardly knowing where I was. Nothing did me any good, and I w4s so weak that I was helpless. All the life seemed to be going out of me.

"It was when the doctor failed that my mother got me some Mother Seigel's Syrup, and that was the first thing to do me any good. It put an end to the wind and headaches, and quieted'my nerves ; my appetite came back, and soon I was quite well again."

Think what suffering Miss Bannister would have avoided had she taken Mother Seigel's Syrup at first. Fully realise this, and you will not neglect the early symptoms of indigestion or biliousness with such a remedy at hand.

lETY DEAR NIECES AND NEPHEWS,

Another week, and such a post-bag! I really think that if the members increase to such an extent as they have done this last month or so I shall have to get a private secre- tary to help with the correspondence. I am unable this week to publish all new contribu- tions, as I must get rid of some of the back matter in justice to my dear relations. Next week I shall have to give a list of names to whom the puzzle prizes have been awarded, and also a long list of new members. I have had several letters from both sexes of the "Corner," showing how that very pretty Japanese fairy tale, " Urashima," was appreciated. I must say that I. myself was quite fascinated by it, and, what's more, I am trying to hunt up another of a similar kind. I must conclude now, as I really cannot afford the space when there are so many more better thiligs remaining to be told by the members. UNCLE NED.

LITTLE GARDENS FOR LITTLE

GARDENERS

I hope that every garden child will have a garden of her very own this year. If you are allowed to choose the place for it I advise you to put it in an open, sunny spot, well away from trees. Let it be in the form of a little border on the east side of the house, or beside a fence, or in front of low shrubbery. It should be about three feet wide, and as long as you are tall. You may think this a very little garden; but it will seem much bigger in the warm days of July and August. Besides, it will grow bigger every year. And I am sure no one needs to be reminded that a little garden well taken care of Is worth far more than a big one filled with weeds and dying plants.

COULDN'T FOOL THE BOY.

A physician was annoyed by a small news- boy, who would run into his office and yell "Evening 'papers?" in a way to startle the patients. Thinking to break him of the habit the doctor stretched a wire across the room, arranging it so that a skeleton hanging in a closet would elide out by pulling a string.

The next day, when the boy appeared In his usual noisy way, the skeleton danced out in front of him, shaking its bones in a most terri- fying manner. The boy gave one yell and dis- appeared down the stairs. Thinking that the trick might have been rather too severe the doctor went down to the street to reasiure the boy, whom he found hiding behind a tree. He called to him to come back, but the boy shook his head.

"No, you don't," he yelled. "I know you if you have got your clothes on."

THE CRACKED COMMANDMENT.

A mother once took her little girl into a shop. While her mother was engaged in another part of the shop, the little girl, feast- ing her eyes on some oranges, nursed the temptation in her heart to taste one, till it grew too strong to be resisted, so she 'hid one under her apron and quickly stole away. But conscience smote her so sharply that after a little reflection she walked as quickly back and slyly replaced the orange in the basket. Again the forbidden fruit out of her possession pre- sented its tempting side, and almost seeming to say, .."Only an orange." Again she yielded. After a%) )harper conflict than before, con- science gained another victory, and the twice- stolen fruit was again and finally restored. With a saddened and ashamed face she walked home with her mother, and when they were alone she buret into tears, sobbing bitterly, "Oh 1 mother, mother, I cracked the com- mandment." "What, my child, broke the commandment?" "No, mother, I didn't break it; indeed, I didn't break it quite; but I'm sure I cracked it."

WHAT THE SCHOOL-BELL SAYS. wonderful what unlike things The school-bell says to the boy when it rings; For instance, the laggard, who drags along On his way to school, hears this sort of song: Oh—suz—hum! Why did I come? Study till four— Books are a bore!

Oh, how I wish I could run off and fiol.l See! There's the brook, Here's a line and a hook. What's that you say? Hurry up--eh? Oh—hum—dm 1 S'pose I must go, Study till four— Books are a bore! Then the boy who loves to be faithful and true, Who does what his parents think best he _ _

should do, Comes bravely along with satchel and books, The breeze in his whistle, the sun in his looks, And these are the thoughts that well up like

a song, As he hears the old.bell with its faithful ding- dong Cling, clang, cling— I'm so glad I could sing! Heaven so blue; Duty to do! Birds in the air, • Everything fair! Even a boy Finds study a joy! When my work's done I'm ready for fun; Keener my play For the tasks of the day. Cling, clang, cling— I'm so glad I could sing! These are the songs which the two boys .heard, When the school-bell was ringing, word for

word. Which do you think was the truer song? Which do you hear as you're trudging along? Don't be a laggard I—far better, I say, To work when you work, and play when you play. 11 4-

COUNTING HER CHICKENS BEFORE THEY

WERE HATCHED,

A country-maid was walking to town with a pail of milk on her head. Thinkina of the money she would get for the sale of her milk, she said to (herself, "I shall be able to buy a large stock of eggs, and, even if some of them should not be good, I shall at least have fifty of them fit to be hatched. Of course, the chickens will cost me a little for their food; but when I sell them at Christmas, I shall get plenty of money to buy myself a new dress." gel mind was 60 full of these pl.s,neant thoughts, that she could not forbear giving a skip of delight, when down came the pail of milk, and all her dreams of happiness were at an end.

From your dear little Niece, MAY SYLVESTER.

THE HIPPOPOTAMIJS

There were once some gentlemen exploring a river in Africa, when all at once they felt a violent shock underneath the boat, and in another moment a huge hippopotamus lifted itself from the water and rushed at the boat with its mouth open. With its monstrous jaws it tore seven planks from the side. It then disappeared, but soon rose again, intending to do the same thing once more. One of the men on board fired at it, and so prevented the attack. The boat rapidly filled and sank, but the crew succeeded in reaching the shores in safety. Probably the bottom of the boat had t-ouched the back of the animal and irritated him to such an extent that he made this fierce attack. MAGGIE EASTWOOD.

ir---,•--- HE WASN'T BLIND.

In a well-known street in London a beggar was often seen plodding about with a small dog. The dog Nyae ,held by a piece of chain, and had round his neck a placard with. "Pity the blind," in large red letters. Mr. T-, passing one day, dropped a six- pence into the man's outstretched hand.

- "Halloa I". he cried, as he WBl3 turning away, "was that a half-eovereign I gave you?" "No, sir—no," answered the beggar; "only sixpence."

"-So," said Mr. T—, "you are not blind, then, after all?"

"Bless you, sir, no!" he renlied. "You see, the placard refers to the dog,. He's blind— not me!"

GRUMBLE TONE

There was a boy named Grumble Tone, who ran

away to -sea. "I'm sick of things on land," he said, "as sick as I can be! A life on the bounding wave will suit a lad like

- me 1" The seething ocean billows failed to stimulate his mirth, For he did not like the vessel, or the dizzy rolling berth, And he thought the sea. was almost as un- pleasant as the earth. The reason was that, north or south, nereer hie steps were bent, On land or sea in court or hall, he found but discontent; For he tool his disposition with him. everywhere he went.

THE FREE CHURCHES.

(By Our London correspondent.)

===== 0002601_19070504_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAY' MEETINGS.

The religious World is in the thick of the May meetings season, and London shows visible signs of the invasion of ministerial and lay delegates, who have left their homes in the Provinces for the feast of oratory provided at the Metropolis every day and night for the next two mouths. It would be difficult to calculate the number of persons attending special meetings just now, and, of course, it would be impossible to report them all.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
BAPTIST UNION MEETINGS.

The denominational bodies commence their annual assemblies at this season. The Bap- tists held their Spring meetings last- week, and next week the Congregational -Union meets. I was only able to attend one of the Sessions of the Baptist Union, but this was quite sufficient to convince .one of the success that has attended .the Baptists this year. They have had a memorable twelve months. under the leadership of that friend of all the Free. Churches, the Rev.. F. B. Meyer, B.A. Mr. Meyer has viitcd his brethren in town and village thrOughout the land, and the Baptists are sorry to lose him as their President. Through the generosity of Mrs. Miner, the Baptist Union has been presented with a por- trait of Mr. Meyer. The Rev. J. H. Shake- speare, the popular secretary, in a very chaste and stirring speech, on behalf of the donor of the pqrtrait, made an appal to him to come. and end his days With his. Baptist brethren. In a moving reply, Mr. Meyer said he always had been, and always would be, a Baptist, and gave some particulars offthe now Ivork he is about to undertake for the Free Churches.' As showing the increasing union that prevails among the Free Churches, it was interesting to observe the welcome accorded to Dr. Rendel Harris, a Friend, and the President of the National Free Church Council, when he gave to the Union one of his unique "lecturettes."

BAPTIST UNION PRESIDENCY.

The new President of the Baptist Union, Principal Henderson, of Bristol College, has secured a strong hold on the denomination. Bristol is the oldest Nonconformist college in the country, and can point to twenty Presi- dents of the Union from among its roll. Mr. Henderson, with his quiet. and unassuming, almost brusque, manner, has captivated the hearts of the students under his charge, and made no light impression on the theological and intellectual life of. the denomination. His successor, nominated according to custom by popular ballot as Vice-President, is an old Bristol man, and pastor of one of the largest churches in North London—the Rev. Charles Brown, of Ferme Park. Mr. Brown, without any of the graces of the mere orator, without attempt at fine phrasing or effect, touches the Leart as few preachers are able to do. As modest as he is earnest, he has for many years been establishing a quiet reputation, both among he Baptists and in the Free Church world 'beyond, and his election to the highest office .in the gift of his denomination ii a well-deserved honour.

THE PEACE CAMPAIGN.

The National, Free Church Council is inau- gurating a campaign to bring into effect the strong resolution passed at the Leeds meetings on the subject of the Hague Conference. At that meeting it -was resolved that special ser- mons should be preached and prayers offered from every Free Church pulpit in support of the proposal to interpose mediation before the breaking out of war. In the United States great meetings have been held, and it has have decided to send delegates to the Govern- ments of Europe in favour of the Peace Crusade: The Hague Conference has vast possibilities of good, but there is always the possibility that the efforts of such an impor- tant gathering may be nullified by influences intangible but subtle. We can but do our best to create such a strong feeling in favour, of peace as shall give a clear mandate to our Government at the Conference. It has been deemed best that we should concentrate on one point—the proposal to interpose mediation before the breaking out of war. -This is a point of the utmost importance, and one which, if gained, should considerably lessen the possibilities of outbreak of war. On Sun- day, June 8, in anticipation of the meetings, the attention of the whole Free Church world will be devoted to this subject.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE CHURCH YEAR BOOK

A publication which has been awaited with much interest has now been issued. "The Free Church Year Book" contains the official report of the animal meetings of the National Free Church Council, together with a review of the year and a whole mass of information invaluable to the Free Churches. It is especially interest- ing to watch the growth of the denominations known as the Free Churches, and to see, in the world survey, how greatly they exceed in num- tw..tl Ennennal Church. The book is issued

by the Publication Department of the National Council at 2s. 6d. net.

OPEN-AIR WORK.

Arrangements are now being made by Free Church Councils all over the country for the starting of Open-Air Services. If the weather is favourable there should be a large increase in this forth of work.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
FRIIE CHURCHMEN IN AMERICA

• Gipsy Smith, the National Council's Evan- gelist, continues his wonderful 'series of mis- sions in America. This month's Erre. Church Chronicle contains a full report of the Brooklyn mission from the pen pf Dr. Cadman. The Rev. Thomas Law, the secretary of the National Free Church Council, is at ,the pre- sent time on a short visit to the United States, where he has had a cordial welcome. It is expected that he will be returning with Giß4''Smith in the middle of May.

FREE CHURCH •SUMMER SCHOOL

One of the .prominent features of Dr. Rendel Harris' year of office in connee tion with the Free Church Council is to be a summer school at Cambridge from July 29 to August 10. A draft programme has been issued by the National Council and can be obtained from the Memorial Hall. There will be devotional meetings and , lectures each morning, and usually a public lecture in the evening. The afternoons will be. devoted to excursions and recreations. Some of the most prominent scholars and theologians of England and the Continent are to assist at these gather- ings. A ticket for the whole period (covering bed, breakfast, lunch, dinner' and lectures) costs £3 15s. The charge for lectures only is 10s. I understand that. all applications must be sent not later than June 30. This new experiment will be watched with considerable interest. Dr. Harris holds a unique place in the Free Church world, and doubtless will give a touch of his own personality to the pro- ceedings. E. J. M.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBA IL

NORTHERN UNION CUP FINAL. Warrington's Great Victory. OLDHAM WELL-BEATEN AT BROUGHTON.

Warrington proved themselves worthy oi the confi- dence reposed in them on Saturday, for they beat Oldham in the Anal for the Northern Union Cup and thus became the only Lancashire Club to have secured the trophy twice. It Was a desperate struggle from start to finish and the better team won. There was a great crowd, 18,500 paying £l,OOB for admission, and the Broughton Rangers' ground was packed. Warrington had a following of quite 5,000 people, most of whom wore club colours and made theniselves heard through- out the afternoon. A rain storm in the early after- noon looked like spoiling the match, but fortunately it cleared before the kick-off and the Contest was fought out under capital conditions. Both teams turned out as selected, viz.:—

Warrington—Tilley ; Fish, Isherwood, Taylor, and Brooks; Lees and Hockonball ; Thoraas,Shugars, Bolton, Boardman, Heath, and Naylor. Oldham—Thomas, Tyson ; W.Dixon, Irvin and White; Lees and Yewlett ; Avery, Ferguson, Smith, Vowles, Tophatn, and Wilkinson. Rtferee, Mr. F. Renton, riunslet. Touch judges, Messrs. J. Priestley, Salford, and J. McGowan, Brough- ton.

Warrington, who had what little advantage there was to be gained by winning the toss, were Ludoubtedly quicker to get going than their opponents, though a penalty for off-side put them on their defence in the first minute. From the first scrummage five yards short of the Warrington line Avery and Topham made a strong rush, but were cleverly pulled up by Tilley, who cleared grandly, and from that point Oldham were a long ti7...-e before they again became really dangerous. The " Wirepullers," whose forwards were playing with desperate energy, had, indeed,the best of matters during the first quarter of an hour, though their intense eager- ness caused them to lose some good opportunities. The forwards put Lees and Hookenhall frequently in posses- sion, but the centres, Isherwood and Taylor, had some bad passes to take ; otherwise they might have been over the line once or twice during the first quarter. The part played by Fish during the initial stages was not a very prominent one, but be put in two long shots at goal from penalties with which he only just failed to score. Once he made a desperate effort to take across- kick of Taylor's but the ball just beat him near the line. Gradually Oldham settled down, and their forwards,who had at first seemed beaten in the packs, appeared to get on terms with their opponents, and, Vo es, Avery, and Topham showed capital foot-work. With the improve- ment in the play of the forwards the Oldham backs got opportunities, and a series of passing movements initiated by Arthur Lees, compelled Warrington to fall back to their own line. Oldham then had a succession of " near things." Thomas, from a penalty, just missed a goal, and a possing bout in which the ball passed through the hands of Yewlett, Dixon, and Tyson, only just failed to bring off a score. The last-named was pushed into touch a foot from the line. Oldham had, . however, got a good position, and from the next scrum- mage Lees broke through and finally passed to Avery, who sewed the first try, which White was unable to convert. Within the next five minutes Warrington bad three penalty kicks, and from the tbird Fish kicked a beautiful goal, which made a difference of only one point in the scores. Thus at half-time the state of the game was :—Oldham,,l try (3 points) ; Warrington, 1 goal (2 points). Elockenhall and Brooks changed places on resuming. There were some desperate efforts to score on the part of both sides. Ferguson twice had shots at goal, but he could not succeed. Neither could Fish on the Warring- ton side. Ai isfielding by one of the Warrington backs very nearly proved disastrous. While the ball was bounding, Yewlett managed to rob Fish of it, and the ! Oldham half dashed down the touch line side in fine I I style, but his object was frustrated by a splendid tackle Iby Tilley. Oa the other side Brooks very nearly let Oldham in, but fortunately for Warrington he was covered by Taylor, whose cool play during the second half was a marked feature of the game. He it was who I on another occasion went down boldly and picked up i I from Topham and Ferguson, and thus saved his side by kicking clear. The second half bad been twenty i minutes in progress before Warrington took the lead . with a converted try. There was quite a dramatic ! element about the score' which followed upon a desperate maul in the mouth of the Oldham goal. In this maul Taylor and Shugars collided, and both had to be carried off to have their heads bandaged. From the very next scrummage, and whilst these players were still off the field, the ball was swung oat by Hockenhall, and after some frantic efforts by Isherwood, Fish and ; Lees, the last-named got over with a try, which Fish I converted. Once having got the lead, Warrington began to play a typical cup-tie game, and kept the ball as close as possible. But Oldham played in desperate fashion, and there were many times when they looked , like making up the leeway. Taylor, however, was very 1 strong and cool on the defence, and it was largely due 1 to him that Oldham could not score. Brooks made a 1 superb dash and just failed to score by grounding the ' ball a few inches short of the line. The venue of , hostilities changed to the other end, where Oldham , were very dangerous. There they must have scored but ' for a couple of very bad passes, which not only lost them the opportunity but also practically lost them the game. The first pass was from Lees to Irvin, who after taking it, threw the ball wildly out towards Tyson. But the had pass Was Fish's opportunity. The Warrington captain bustled Tyson off the ball and in a trice put in one of those individual efforts for which he is famous, and scored a grand try. What he did was to dribble the ball three parts the length of the field, and then complete the dribble with a run in which he easily swer,ved round Thomas. The Warrington captain also added the goal points. All was now over so far as Oldham were concerned, but before the end came Hockenhall completed the War- rington triumph by kicking over Thomas' bead and scoring another try after a long run, which Fish ' con- verted. Result :—Warrington 4 'goals 3 tries (17 points), Oldbani 1 try (3 points.) !

===== 0002601_19070504_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESENTATION OF THE CUP.

Immediately the match concluded there was a rush I to the members' stand, where the ceremony of present- ing the cup to the winning team had been arranged to take place. Mr. J. B. Cooke, the President of the Union, in addressing the crowd, said that for the first time, he ! believed, m the history of the Northern Union, four I Lancashire clubs had played in the semi-finals. Whether that was owing to the draw or to their superiority on I the football field it was not for him to say. Most of those present would, however, agree that the semi-finals had brought into the field two teams fully capable of playing a fine game of football. (Applause.) His sYm- pathy went forth to the Oldham team because, though they had played exceedingly well throughout the season. they had finally fallen between two stools. At the same time he must congratulate the Warrington team upon their display that day, and upon the fact that in three Previous seasons they had taken part in the final cup tie in one of which they were victorious. He sincerely hoped that both teams would have as successful a season next year as they had had this. (Applause). The cup was then presented to the Warrington cap- tain by Mrs. Cook. On receiving it Fish offered his best respects to the Warrington followers, and expressed

the hope that Warrington would again win the trophy while he was playing. Su'osequently the officials and visitors were enter- tained to dinner by the Northern Union at the Grosvenor Hotel, Manchester.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
WORKS COMPETITION FINAL.

LOCOS V. RYLANDS.

The final of the Warrington Works Competition attracted a record crowd to the Wilderspool ground on Monday, and an excellent game was witnessed. From the start the game was of an exciting nature, and early on Rylands bad the best of matters, though they missed several easy chances. About ten minutes after the start they rushed the ball over the line, and there over- ran it, with the result that the Locos' full back was able to touch down. Tbe only 'score up to the interval was a penalty goal to Rylands, which was splendidly kicked by Gibbs. On changing ends Rylands began to supply pressure, and from a scrimmage close to the line Floyd scored cleverly.under the posts, Phillips convert- ing. •The Locos attacked with vigour, Anderton scoring at the corner, and after Daintith had kicked a goal from a penalty, a forward movement resulted in the ball being grounded over the line, Daintith converting. Close on time the same player dropped a splendid goal, and the Locos ran out winners by 12 points to 7. , At the conclusion of the match the cup and medals were presented to the winners by Mr. J. E. Warren, president of the club. The Northern Union Cup, whioh was won by Warrington on Saturday, was also on view. Mr. H. ASHTON, the popular secretary of the War- rington Club, explained that the Competition was instituted for the encouragement of junior football in IWarrington. Some two years ago a junior rugby league was formed but unfortunately it had come to an , untimely end and .the Committee of the Warrington' Club had decided that the cup which Messrs. Eustance presented to the League should be held for twelve , months by the winners of the Works' Competition. (Hear, hear.) Mr. WARREN said he shared the general rejoicing at the success of the Warrington Club in the Northern Union Cup Competition just as he would have shared their disappointment had they lost. Referring to the Works' Competition he said the first object was a noble one and was to raise money to devote to charitable objects in the town. The other object was perhaps not so creditable and was to encourage rugby football and to' get players to recruit the ranks of the Warrington Club. The latter object had not been the success which he should have liked and it did seem a pity to him that the teams which took part in that competition should be got together to play a few times and then be disbanded, kr twelve months. He suggested that if possible a league should be formed amongst those clubs. He knew there were difficulties in the way of expense bathe believed tho4e could be overcome, and he thought the thing would he a success if only ten of the 33 clubs which had taken part formed a league. He was pleased to see that firms like Rylands' Bros. and Messrs. Cros- fields were taking an interest in athletics and were pro- viding recreation grounds for their employees and he hoped that other works would follow their example. (Hear,hear). The receipts in the year's competition had established a record. Some £164 bad been taken and, after deducting expenses there was left something like £lO4 to give to charities and injured players. (Applause). Mr. WARREN then, amidst cheers, presented the medals. Councillor NEILD proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Warren for his services. _ _ . .

Alderman EVANS seconded and the vote was accorded with great heartiness. Cries for Fish and Isherwood were then raised and the greatest enthusiasm was displayed as the two players responded.

THE CAPTAIN Aria .THE CUP.

(J. Fish.)

llockenhall practically repeated Fish's feat on the opposite wing, for he slipped through along the touch line, kicked over Thomas's head, and after running about 60 yards scored a magnificent try, Fish kicked his fourth goal, and the Cup was woa!

I metaphorically raise my hat to the Oldham men. They had made a gallant fight and to lose both the League cup and the Northern Union cup in successive weeks was cruel misfortune. The side was not seen at its best, but there is no doubt that the Warrington men had a bit up their sleeves for this match. They had more pace and more dash in the closing stages, and they kept their heads better. I have no hesitation in awarding the palm to the two Widnes lads, Taylor and Tilley, who both played an heroic game and saved the citadel repeatedly. Fish was not in his best form on the whole, but his brilliant try and fine goal- kicking proved once again his inestimable value to the side. 'Eleven points out of the seventeen was his share ! Brooks was better at half than wing—in fact both he and Hockenhall proved more effective when they changed places. Sammy Lees played his best game against Oldham, while Isherwood was reliable as ever. The forwards were splendid, and none was more noticeable than the burly Shugars who showed sur- prising speed at times. Thomas fielded and kicked cooly I and cleverly and was as good as an extra back. ' There was not much individual cleverness shown on the Oldham side and the team seemed better adapted to defence than attack. A. Lees played a good game and so did the wings, but both centres and full back were slow. Fergnsson was a remarkable failure as a goal-kicker. In both the League and Cup finals he never once found the mark. After such a brilliant season, " Fergy's " failure is cruel irony.

I should like to add a word of praise to the work of the referee, M. F. Renton. Though probably neither side would agree with all his decisions, yet he was remarkably quick, firm and fair. He gave one of the best examples of really competent refereeing I have seen in a big match, and in a season of so much complaint about the referees, his exhibition of when and how to whistle was gratifying.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CUP FlisIAL.

Sensational Finish at Broughton.

Lily " Ovallst."]

Evidently, Warrington possess a great football team, for to appear four times in the Northern Union Cup Final and carry off the trophy tivice in six years is an achievement which any club may be justly proud of. Though there was plenty of faith in the local men last Saturday, the prowess of the men from Oldham was admitted, and a harder task could not have been set the Wirepullers. That they came through the ideal so gloriously is infinitely to their credit, and to the credit also of the officials and committee wlr) have been responsible for the management of the team. Warrington possess the additional honour of being the only Lancashire club to have wog the N.U. trophy twice. The Wirepullers —as they are still nicknamed throughout the North —may fairly claim to be the champions of professional Rugby football. They have always been renowned as Cup- fighters, and it is gratifying to find that the prowess of old is maintained and added to by the present genera- tion. There is. no sign of decadenee here, and I believe Warrington's motto is the appropriate one of "Excelsior." Long may the "primrose and blue" be to the fore!

The scenes at Broughton on Saturday will long live in the memory of the Warrington football enthusiasts. 'They never witnessed a more desperately fought, thrilling and sensational final. There were full five thousand of them, and if they were happy and con- Ident before the contest, they were deliriously joyful at the close. " Wire " had withstood an enormous tensile strain without weakening, and we were proud of it. When one looks back on the contest, it seems nothing short of a marvel how human endurance could survive the strain. The pace of the game from the start was terrific and it soon became obvious that physical fitness Would have a big say in the final verdict. For a long time no man could have foretold the winners. "Jack "was as good as his master, and in the end a bit better, but only in the end. There was no question about the fitness of the Warrington men and in the last stages of the contest they were fresher than the hard-worked Oldham thirteen, and that extra freshness had much to do with the sadden and climactic outburst of scoring. Nevertheless the score of 17 points to 3 is unduly flattering to the -winners. Oldham were much nearer winning the Cup than the figures suggest, and only the Warring- ton men know what a load was taken from their backs when Fish scored the electrifying try which broke the hearts of the men from Spindledom and settled the issue. But of this anon.

It needs to be remembered—in order to appreciate the desperate conditions—that Oldham held a small lead for nearly three-fourths of the game. Moreover, seven minutes from time they were only four points in arrears and attacking with desperate energy. But these seven minutes became memorable To take events in their proper sequence, I must admit that at the end of the first quarter Warrington shoul.l have been six or eight points up, but over-eagerness caused chances to go begging. At the end of the second quarter, Oldham ought to have had a score of some- thing like nine points instead of three, for the great Fergusson and White missed goal kicks which caused the Oldham supporters to perspire profusely in an agony of mind. Even the three points did not con- stitute a lead at half time, for Fish wiped off a couple with a beautiful goal which showed that one man at least had the right pair of boots on.

It is only fair to admit that Oldham had the best of matters up to the interval. And for another quarter of an hour they continued to hold an advantage. But though they pressed, there was not the cleverness nor the dash necessary to break down the Warrington defence, and some of the tactics suggested that there was an opinion in the Oldham mind that a lead of even one point was enough. The error was soon proved. Warrington, as the game wore on, seemed to freshen, and just at three-quarter time there was a terrific assault on the Oldham line. Taylor and Shuga.rs collided in a frantic dash for the ball, and both were on the touch line for the next ten minutes. But with such depleted forces Warrin,gton got the ball from the Oldham forwards and in a trice it was passed along to Fish. The latter was hemmed in, but ru,hing to the line he twisted round and passed to Lees just as he was tackled. The ex-Oldham half snapped the chance and then enjoyed the crowning moment of his career, when he found himself safely over the line. What a roar and a rattle there was I The crowd fairly rose at the plucky Warrington men, and it seemed but fitting when Fish carefully placed a goal. Still the two injured combatants were in the hands of the medicine men and matters were critical when Oldham bore down on the Warrington goal. But first Shucrars and then Taylor dashed into the fray, the last-named with bandages and a.skull-cap that testified to the serious- ness of his injuries. It was now that the test of Warrington's resource and endurance commenced. The Oldham men were frantically eager to score, and even the veteran Arthur Lees made errors of judgment. On the contrary, Warrington kept their heads, and the cool defensive play of Taylor arid Tilley at this stage was beyond all praise. The line of defenders never wavered—and then came the climax. Oldham had kept the ball as much from Fish's side as possible throughout the game•but in the excitement of an attack at such close quarters they forgot their dangers. The ball was passed out passesl with more speed than cleverness and between them Irvin and Tyson mulled. All at once a streak of primrose and blue flashed from the ruck and Fish was seen to come away with the ball at his toes. For a few yards he was within reach of his pursuers,and then the weeks of preparation and the Warrington idol's natural speed told. Fish drew clear every stride and as he passed the stand at a gallop he was four yards in front of Tyson and the ball nicely within reach. Thomas, the Oldham back came across with a rush but he had a football genius to encounter. At the critical moment ish scooped the dancing ball up with the left hand, swerved beautifully inside, and left Thomas looking helplessly on while he cantered under the posts with a try that was positively electrifying. The scorer goaled again, and the Warrington men were on velvet with the score 12 3. There were only four minutes left, hut Oldham made another effort and sustained another reverse.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
RETURN OF THE TEAM.

The return of the team was not accompanied by the scenes of uncontrolled enthusiasm which were witnessed two years ago, though considerable excitement prevailed. A large crowd assembled to welcome the victors at the Central station and the appearance shortly before half-past eight, of Fish, holding the cup in a loving embrace, was the signal for an outburst of cheering. Two buses were in waiting,the borough band being accommodated on the first, whilst the second was occupied by the tearii and officials,and to the appropriate tune of" See the conquering heroes " a start was made for the procession round the town. The route was thickly lined with spectators who indulged in hearty cheers and in the street bands of irrepressible youths wearing the club colours marched up and down singing or rather murdering, popular airs to the accompaniment of the hideous combination of sound produced by their operations on rattles, tin whistles, and in fact anythino from which a noise could be extracted. A disappoint- ment awaited the team in that no official wel- come was extended to them by the Corporation. They had proceeded to the Town Hall only to find the building in darkness and the steps crowded with lusty lunged juveniles, and after a short halt the journey was continued. Generally speaking, the towns- people were content at having viewed the cup which the Captain of the team throughout held on high, but the more enthnsiastic kept up the rejoicings until a late hour. and midnight had passed ere the revellers returned to their homes. MR. CROSFIELD AND THE MATCH.

On Friday Mr. Crosfield wired as follows to Mr. Pemberton :—" Very many thanks for invitation to join party at Warrington but two engagements to-night oblige me to travel direct to London-road, Manchester, arriving there 2.3 to-morrow. Greatly hope we may bring back cup, delighted to hear team is in fine form. Hope we may look forward to great triumph to-morrow." Later Mr. Crosfleld had to wire to inform Mr. Pemberton that a suddEn illness prevented his attendance at the match and in a letter which he forwarded he said:— How do you intend to celebrate Saturday"; big victory? If anything is done I should be most glad to lend a helping hand, financially or otherwise, or both. It was really bad luck that this attack of feverish catarrh should lay hold of me on Friday night. I suppose I caught a chill in the thick yellow fog on Friday morn- ing or in the heavy rain after it. It is years since the last dose and I am most mortified and disappointed that this should have happened just at a time to prevent me seeing the great game and the splendid triumph of our fellows. At the same time we are all immensely proud of the victory and I hope in some way or other that steps 0111 be taken to do honour to the team and that in a small way T may be allowed to assist. Mr. Crostield wired after the match :—" Please con- vey to cup team best congratulations on truly magnifi- cent victory. Hope to have early opportunity of expresFing this personally." Other telegrams were received by Mr. Ashton from Huddersfield Rugby Football Club, Oldham players and officials; Steve Smith, Penarth, a former player of the Warrington Club; Mr. Percy Adams, Birmtnghare, a prominent Rugby Unionist.—Mr. Robert Pierpoint wired : "Convey to football team my best congratula- tions on victory."

What other People Think. THE GENIES OF FISII.

" Flaneur "in the" Leeds Murcury " says: Leaving out of account the last thrilling ten minutes, the game was one of the, poorest finals in Northern Union history. Indeed, I should class it as absolutely the worst, with the single exception of the Halifax and Salford final at Headingley in 1903. I have no doubt that ne,trly everyone had been say- ing" chis is not the Fish of old," throughout the game, for the famous left wing had shown a great want of thought in his dropping out, and had been playing in a very lackadaisical fashion. There had been, indeed, nothing to distinguish hip from the very mediocre club three-quarter, and one had come to the conclusion that Fish had had his day, when his great chance came. Perhaps he had been waiting for some lqig opportunity. When it came he showed the old touch of genius, and scored a try that perhaps no other man in the field could have made possible.

THE LUCK AND. THE REFEREE

I'The Oldham Evening Chronicle" says : All the luck ras with Oldham's opponents. Everything they Idid came right. and Oldham could do nothing rigEt, whilst they were unfortunate in the matter of incurring penalties. I have not the remotest intention of ques- tioning Mr. Renton's bona fides, but he certainly was r extremely severe on Oldham for trivial offences, and in the case of the off-side rule he frequently penalised Oldham, where their side had given the Warrington player fully the required distance before playing, whilst on the other hand he noticeably neglected to penalise the Wirepullers for the same offence and others. On one occasion, certainly, he did order the ball back when

Heath seemed to have scored 'for Warrington, but frequently he allowed them to profit by breaches of the rules. Two of their tries were gained under these cir- cumstances. Sam Lees received the ball from a forward pass when he scored, and when Fish scored his sensa- tional try he ought to have been stopped before he had gone a yard. He and Tyson were both going for the ball together, Tyson being inrfront of him, and, seeing , that the Oldham man was certain to get it, Fish deliber- I ately charged him in the back and flung him, although he had not the ball in his possession.

ANOTHER OLDHAM OPINION.

The" Oldham Standard "says : After Oldham having had so much the best of the game, it almost appears incredible that they should be beaten by such a score. The score, however, by no means represents the game. Warrington were the luckiest team in the world to escape a score against them when Fish scored for them. Fortune appeared to have deserted Oldham with a vengeance, and whatever luck there was knocking about fell Warrington way. Apart from that, the game was worth going miles to see despite the adverse criticism of a certain Manchester contemporary. I was not very greatly impressed with the Oldham attack; in fact, that was just where the team was let down.

===== 0002601_19070504_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EAR-WRACKING DIN.

The" Manchester Guardian "says :—The turbulent noisiness of Deansgate compared with the pandemonium at the crowded football ground was as the murmur of a summer sea to the roar of rock-torn surges in a tempest. Every tenth Warrington man seem to possess a rattle, and lifting arms as well as voices they made the most ear-wracking din that was ever raised by high- spirited football enthusiasts packed together in a lump. From the moment the players appeared until the match was over there was a continuous raging of strange sounds, and when the Warrington side secured their commanding advantage towards the end of the game the crash of the tumult reached a frenzied crescendo that seemed to impel individual men to do strangely eccentric things, as. for example,to smash umbrellas and walking-sticks on the roof of the stands with a deliberate spirit of destrtlctiveness which meant only that human speech and human voice failed to express sufficiently the delirious delight of the moment. Many during the football season that is gone have uttered jeremiads on the Northern Union game ; , they have spoken of it is a thing with its fortunes in the grave, and so on. But Saturday showed that it is yet a game that can bring together 20,000 people and give them cause for great demonstrations of high spirits and good humour even one cold and drizzling afternoon.

A YORKSHIRE CRITICISM.

The" Yorkshire Post" says :—While the match had its fine individual performances, as here described, it is quite impossible for any Rugby man of knowledge, and experience to pretend to be enthusiastic over the class of football which the game produced. In the first half one seemed to be viewing, one of the worst features of the handling code—that of fierce and indis- criminate rushing and charging—witliout its compensa- tions in skilful handling and quick combined running. There was an improvement in the second half, when the ardour of some of the men had partly played itself out, but even then there was far too much punting and "marking" and wild rushing, and but for the great efforts of iFish and Hockenhall the matoh would have had to be spoken of as having had little else but rush and excitement to recommend it.

WARRINGTON'S FITNESS.

The "Manchester Courier ":—The Watersheddings team did the most pressing for half-way through the second half, but then it was seen that Warrington were in possession of that reserve strength necessary in such a strenuous struggle. Cup-tie games are not as a rule good expositions of football, but this was one of the best of its kind. Oar Yorkshire friends considered it a poor game, but what else could one expect from them when only Lancashire clubs were engaged?

The" Liverpool Courier" :—One mast acknowledge that Warrington were the fitter set, and too much praise cannot be bestowed on the committee in having their men so fit for such an important contest. The big, lusty forwards were especially nippy, and they were seen to greatest advantage in the closing stages. The superiority of the " Wirepullers "was demonstrated to the full, and the confidence of the backs was in direct contrast to that of the opposition.

MR. I. T. WILLIAMS.

Promoted from Warrington to London.

We understand that during the present week a somewhat sudden change has come about in the management of the London and North-Western Railway at Warrington. From the Ist inst Mr. I. T. Williams, the genial and indefatigable goods manager of the War- rington district, has been promoted to the important and responsible position of superintendent of the London Goods Stations and of the Goods and Parcels Agency in London. As our readers will doubtless remember,Mr. Williams came from Abergavenny four years ago, where he had for several years acted as assistant goods manager in the South Wales District. He then came to Warrington as a complete stranger, but by his winning manner he soon made many friends and gained the confidence of those placed under his charge in the ‘Varrington Dis- trict, so that whilst cordially offering him our con- gratulations on his well merited promotion, we are sure that railways employes of all grades, as well as the large circle of friends outside the railway Service, will deeply regret the loss sustained by his removal from the district.

Mr. kVilliams was ever ready and willing to do a good turn to anyone who appealed to him for help, and therefore his departure will leave a void which will not be easily filled. . . . _ . _ _ _

The work he has done for the betterment of the staff and railwaymen generally at Warrington, in addition to his onerous railway iuties, is really wonderful. Soon after his advent into Warrington, he turned his atten- tion to the provision of better office accommodation for his personal staff and after strenuous effort, he was successful in getting the fine building at Arpley Station fitted up as headquarters for the district, ant' what the London and North-Western Railway Company's chief goods manager, Mr. Frank Ree, recently des- cribed as the grimy old offices at Bank Quay were beautified and turned into a Railwaymen's Insti- tute, where all grades of the service can enjoy them- selves after a hard day's work. In addition to this, owing to the untiring efforts of Mr. Williams, a bowling green and miniature shooting range were only a fort- night ago opened by Mr. and Mrs. Frank Ree, who have shown a great and kindly interest in all that has been done for the benefit of railwaymen. All this has been accomplished in the short space of four years, so that the subject of our sketch has left his mark, and all departments in the railway service will have good reason to bless the day that brought him into Warrington- Whilst all will rejoice at his promotion, many regrets are expressed that he has so soon been taken away from us, and that he has not been permitted to enjoy the fruits of his labours in Warrington. We are informed that Mr. Williams has been succeeded by Mr. F. A. Sargeant, of London, who it is hoped will continue the good work that his predecessor has begun

JAPANESE BAZAAR.

PICTURESQUE SCENE IN THE PARR HALL. .

Great Effort by Warrington Wesleyans.

It would seem a far cry from the streets of Warring- ton to the sunny land of Japan, particularly on such a cheerless day as Tuesday. The transformation, how- ever, was easier than at first sight it might appear, for on entering the Parr Hall the townsman in search of change of scene could almost imagine himself trans- ported to some delighted Eastern city. Beautiful gardens, picturesquely built houses, and the circular bridges peculiar to Japan helped to make up a unique spectacle, while ladies in Japanese costumes flitted hither and thither, endeavouting to dispose of the works of art mid industry with which the houses, otherwise stalls, were profusely stocked. It was a happy idea for the congregation of Bold-street Wesleyan Church to hit upon the representation of a "garden city of Japan" for their long anticipated bazaar, as the scenic effects in themselves constitute a great attraction. The venture has been taken up with splendid enthusiasm, and the financial success has surpassed all expectations. The object of the bazaar was particularly commendable and the way in which all the Wesleyans of Warrington have rallied to the support of the Bold-street congregation in their great effort has been worthy of all praise. The scheme which the bazaar will make certain of realization includes the liquidation of the whole of the church debts, the erection of a new organ, and the renovation and decoration of the church. At least a sum of £1,700 is required, and before the bazaar the treasurers had in cash and promises £630. It was hoped to raise £l,OOO by the bazaar, and the balance of £7O at the opening services.

The Rev. JOSEPH MINSING, the indefatigable presi- dent of the bazaar committee, has done all in his power to further the project and he has been ably supported by an enthusiastic band of offi.cers. Mr. W. Silcock and Mr. Reginald H. Silcock have fatfilled the duties of hon treasurers and Hr. Frank A. Harris and Mr. Isaac E. Barlow have been most energetic in the discharge of the secre- tarial functions.
