Ice Cream: Master Roy Silcock. THE OPENING CEREMONY.

There was a large attendance at the opening i cere- mony, which was performed by Mrs. Dodds n the absence through illness, of her husband, Mr. James Dodds. The Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) presided, and amongst those present on the platform were :—The Mayoress, Mrs. Dodds, the Misses Dodds, the Rev. S. Pitt (superintendent of the Circuit), the Rev. Joseph Manning (president of the Bazaar Committee), the Rev. John Gunnell, Mr. F. A. Harris, Mr. T. E. Barlow, Mr. Reginald H. Silcock, Mr. Percy Silcock, Mr. John Taylor, Mr. William Hickman, Mr. Pattison, Dr. Bennett, Mr. James Parkinson, Mr. Hedley Taylor. The hymn "0 worship the King" having been sung, The Rev S. PITT expressed the great pleasure it afforded them to cordially welcome the chief magistrate of the borough, and the Mayoress, not only because of the distinguished positions they held but because of the respect and affection entertained for them. (Hear, I hear.) They were pleased to know that the Mayor was in full sympathy with a work of that kind and that he I not only held the high position of chief magistrate but rendered devoted service to the Church to which he belonged. (Applause.) The Rev. J. MANNING said he extended, on behalf of the members of the congragation,a very hearty welcome to the Mayor and Mayoress. They were delighted with the official recognition which their presence gave to the place which Wesleyans occupied in the life and work of the town. They were also delighted to see the Mayor present as a member of another church, a loyal member of the Church of England. It had .been their good fortune, and still was their good fortune, to he on very friendly terms with members of all churches. It was Wesley's motto that "They were the friends of all and the enemies of none," and they in the twentieth century were doing their best to live up to that tradition. He would like to say that those present had come to help a people who were doing their best to help themselves. The appeal which was being made to the public was not to make up for any lack of effort on the part of their own people, but to supplement the hearty efforts of those who were identified with the church and congre- gation in Bold-street. He could assure them that he had never had to do wM.II a scheme that had given him so much pleasure as the present scheme, as such hearti- ne.s, such co-operation, such unanimity and enthusiasm had been displayed which augured well for the success of the bazaar. The object which they had in view was the liquidation of the whole of the church debt, the erec- tion of a new organ and the renovaticin of the church. The whole scheme involved an expenditure of £1,700, towards which sum they had in hand £930 in cash or promises. They hoped by ,the present effort to raise £l,OOO, more if they could get it, of course—(laughter) —because after they had completed the scheme there was something still to be done. One of his earliest superintendents used to say to him, "Once persuade the Wesleyan people of the reasonable- ness of anything you ask them and they will do it "and he thought that, generally speaking, that was the truth. (Applause). He wanted to persuade them that they could very well spend considerably above the £l,OOO they had set themselves to get, andl they could profitably spend it in restoring the stone work of their fine old sanctuary. (Hear, hear.) They were delighted to see present the oldest member of the congregation, Mr. Pattison—(applause) a fine, hale man of 94—(applause)—who could still walk I a considerable distance on Sunday to attend a ' place of worship, and who had during the past few I months collected £lO. (Applause). Mrs. Gandy handed I him (Mr. Manning) as he came on to the plat- form the proceeds of her collection amongst her friends, which amounted to £2l. (Applause.) He had received a telegram from the Rev. Theodore Bishop, a former minister of the circuit, wishing them all every success, while Mrs. Robert Pierpoint had written regretting the enforced absence of her husband. They all regretted Mr. Pierpoint's absence as well as the cause.—(hear, hear)—and rejoiced in the fact that he was being restored to health. Alluding to the bazaar, the rev, gentleman said they were starting under very happy circumstances, but they had one regret

and that was that Mr. Dodds was unable to be with them. They were extremely sorry to miss him, and wished him a speedy restoration to health and stren*th. (Hear, hear, and applause). Mr. Dodds took a practical interest in the work of the church and had done a great deal for them; he had also greatly interested himself in the present effort, having sent his best wishes and a cheque for £lOO. (Hearty applause). They first of all asked Mr. W'm. Walker, of Whitehaven,who though com- pelled to decline, had through Mr. Dodds' influence sent them £5O. (Applause). The Rev. J. Manning, in con- clusion, thanked Mrs. Dodds for her kindness in con- senting to open the bazaar and said he trusted that .havinc, made such a splendid start the effort would- be a grand success. (Applause). The MAYOR, who was cordially received, said he was a Churchman, but he was a broad Churchman, and he believed that everyone who was working for his Maker with a true heart was doing good. Those who had assembled that day were trying to do good for the old Wesleyau Church in Bold-street. He was not a stranger among those in that room or to many Wesleyans outside. He had always been in close touch with the Wesleyan body since he was a boy,and when he heard Mr. Manni,ng read the list of donations, he could say with all sincerity "Well done, good old Bolton." Mr. Dodds had been a friend of his ever since he (the speaker) had known what friendship was—long before he came to Warrington. He knew Mr. Walker by 'repute, and he knew what he had done for Bolton in assisting to build a grand institution there where work- ing men and working women could meet for social evenings, lectures and concerts. He must express his deep regret that Mr. Dodds was unable to be present. He (the Mayor) happened to be in the secret. Some- time ago he met Mr. Dodds who said to him" You are going to our bazaar, and I suppose you will be chair- man." His reply was "If they say so I suppose I must." (Laughter.) Because they knew that the Mayor of a borough had to be commanded. He hoped Mr. Dodds would soon be restored to pesfect health. (Hear, hear.) In conclusion, his Worship expressed the hope that over £l,OOO would be raised as the result of the present effort. (Applause.) Mrs. DODDS said: lam extremely sorry that you have to put up with a makeshift this afternoon, but Mr. Manning has told you why. After all you know that it is an ill-wind that blows nobody any good, and so upon me has fallen the honour of opening this bazaar. You know that I always regard it as a great honour to do anything I can for Wesleyan Methodism. (Applause.) I trust that this is going to be a record bazaar not only from a financial point of view.but also that because of the enthusiasm, and the self-sacrifice that Mr. Manning has been speaking about there shall come to this church a new spirit of love and power. The church wants money and we should think there was something radically , wrong if it did not,—(laughter)—but it certainly wants something as well as money—men and women with strong faith and Christ-like lives, who are willing and ready to use in the service of God and their fellow-men the talents He has given to them. I trust that this bazaar will in every way be a very great success, and I have very great pleasure in declaring it open. (Applause.) Nfr. JOHN TAYLOR, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mrs. Dodds and the Mayor, said Mrs. Dodds had honoured them with a very graceful speech, and Mr. •Dodds by his handsome donation had given them a splendid start. He trusted everyone present would do their utmost to make the bazaar a success. (Applause). Mr. REGINALD H. SILCOCK seconded, and said he had ' never known such enthusiasm as that which was dis- played in connection with the present bazaar. It was an enthusiasm that seemed to pervade all, and which could not fail to make the bazaar a great success. (Applause). It was not the rule,to spend money before they bad it, but they had done so, in this case— (laughter)—and they were, therefore, bound to make an effort to pay what they owed. However, with such beautiful articles on sale they would be able to pay their debts ancl be able with one united voice to sing at the end of the bazaar their Hallelujah. (Applause.) The motion was enthusiastically agreed to, the Mayor and Mrs. Dodds briefly responding. Tuesday's proceedings realized the handsome sum of 2611. WEDNESDAY'S CEREMONY. There was again a large attendance at the opening ceremony on Wedneseay which was performed by Mr. C. E. Parker. Alderman Bennett presided and amongst those present on the platform were Mrs. Bennett, Mr. and Mrs. C. E. Parker, Revs. W. Mellor, J. Gunnell, !J. F. Matthews, John Yonge, F. Vaughan and E. J. Gawne, Councillor Benson, Messrs. F. A. Harris, J. Fairhurst, W. Hickman, A. Silcock, J. E. Richardson and G. Ashton.

The Rev. J. MANNING announced the results of Tuesday's sales. There was, he said,every prospect of their reaching £l,OOO. (Hear, hear.) There still remained a very necesssry work, the restoring of the stone work of their fine old sanctuary, and when they had• raised the, £l,OOO they might as well go on and raise another £3OO for the stone work. (Hear, hear.) He had to announce the receipt of a donation of £5O from Mr. C. E. Parker, (Applause.) It was particularly appropriate that they should that day have Mr. Parker to open the bazaar as he represented one of the oldest Methedist families in the neighbourhood. (Hear, hear.) ' Alderman BENNETT expressed the pleasure it gave him to take part in proceedings • under such charming surroundings and supported by a minister so able and enthusiastic as Mr. Manning and by such a faithful body of workers who had rallied round them. (Hear, hear.) He was particularly pleased to be associated with Mr. Parker.- He (Alderman Bennett) sometimes thought he was the busiest man in Warrington but whenever he was inclined to come to that conclusion he always took comfort in the thought that Mr. Parker was even busier. How Mr. Parker found time for his mtiltitudinous activities he failed to understand. Mr. Parker moreover had a very generous heart and was ever anxious and eager to do all he could to promote the best interests of the town and neighbourhood. He was reminded, by their surroundings that he was associated with Mr. Parker in a scheme to promott, a series of garden suburbs to surround the old town. They had hoped to make Warringaon itself agarden but if they could not do that they would girdle the town with gardens, and he could only hope that they would succeed in making them as beautiful as the garden city of Japan. (Applause.) They had at any rate filled their eyes with visions. Mr. Parker's interest in that movement was only one of the numerous claims which he had upon the gratitude of the people. The' results of the previous day's proceedings were almost a record in the opening of bazaars,and were very encouraging in that they gave the assurance that they would attain their object, and probably raise even more than they asked for before the bazaars closed. The object which they had in view was an excellent one. He was sure they were all anxious to see their fine old chapel beautiful internally and externally—he hoped they would not forget the outside. (Applause). He had seen the work in the interior, and he was delighted, and did not think that he had ever seen a Methodist Chapel look more beautiful. (Hear, hear.) They wanted to make the exterior strong and sound, and to renovate the stone work which was perishing from neglect. (Hear, hear). It afforded him a melancholy pleasure to announce that the trustees under the will of the late Mr. E. Richardson had in accord with the wish of their father forwarded a donation of £25 towords the restoration of the stone work of the Chapel. (Applause). Unless he was mis- informed John Wesley preached in a chapel in Bank- street, and, at any rate, they had a famous and historic record that for probably considerably over a century Methodism had stood for everything which was good and elevating in the town of Warrington. (Hear, hear). It had of late been a serious and great grief to him to see Bold-street not nearly so full as it ought to be. (Hear, hear.) He had no sympathy with the excuse they beard so often urged that everyone had gone to live at Stockton Heath. There were quite sufficient people left in Warrington. A great proportion of the 70,000 people in the town did not go to any place of worship at all and it struck him that there was something e Wrong that all their Churches and Chapels should, comparatively speaking, be so empty. They wanted to adapt their religion to the times. The gospel of Christ was broad enough for all times and situations. They wanted to make their Churches as beautiful as they could and emphasise the fundamental teaching of Christ which sometimes in the wrangle and jangle of the creeds they were apt to forget. They must get to the bed rock bottom principles and make this world a little Heaven below. Christ's teaching carried out properly did not concern itself only with the future state but amongst the ideals would be the making of the cities of the world not only beautiful, healthy and clean but a foretase of the city in Heaven towards which they journeyed. (Applause.) Mr. PARKER said he felt somewhat oppressed by the mention which had been made of the subscriptions which had been given. He supposed it was necessary to announce the amounts but he felt that gifts had been made in work and service which far out-weighed any- thing which had been given by people like himself who possibly gave very freely but to. whom the amount of the gift did not really mean much deprivation. He knew Alderman Bennett would mention garden shburbs, and he believed that with their help Alderman Bennett ,would surround the town with beauty .spots. (Hear, hear.) He had lately been read- ing the new theology and he would like to bring one thought before them. In the introductory chapters Mr. Campbell said the masses of the people on the one hand and the cultured classes on the other were increas- ingly departing from the religion of the Churches. There was a great deal of truth in that, though it was to some extent exaggerated, and it was well that they should be ready to face the truth. Mr. Campbell said it was an undoubted fact that the Churches and creeds were losing their hold upon the modern mind but the name of Jesus was held in greater regard than ever. The point of that was that it was only as the Churches were true to the great central principles of the life of Christ that the Churches would advance and take hold of the world and do their work and in as much as that desire was represented in that bazaar, in the self-denial which had been shown, he was sure thy all wiehed it every success. (Applause.) Mr. WEAVER proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. and Mrs. Parker and Alderman Bennett for their _presence.

This was seconded by Mr. W. HicKmAN and heartily accorded. _ _

Brief replies by Mrs. PARKEitand Alderman BENNETT concluded the speechmaking. Wednesday's sales amounted to £3ll.

CHILDREN'S DAY.

PRETTY SCENES AT THURSDAY'S OPENING. Thursday was "children's day" and the opening ceremony for the occasion was both novel and attractive. Mrs.H.Roberts presided and was supported by Mrs. Reginald Silcock, Mrs. Withinshaw, Revs. J. Manning and E. J. Gawne, NLessrs. R. H. Silcock, W. Isaac, F. Pendlebury, J. Lingard, J. Doncaster, J. T. Pattison, J. E. Barlow, J. Bayley, B. Bennett, T.Prince, J. Robinson and W. Silcock.

Mrs. ROBERTS said they all felt that the children could not be taught too early that it was one of their privileges to work for the church to which they belonged. (Applause.) They all knew the objects of the bazaar and knew how Bold-street Church had been beautifully decorated, and she was glad to know that it was to be further enriched by the provision of a new and beautiful organ. She often heard her mother say that she had a brick in Bold-street Church. as, when a young woman, she worked for a bazaar held to promote the building of the sanctuary in the days of the Rev. George Dickenson. They had had two success- ful days and she wished that that day might be a record. (Applause.) Here there was a break in the speech-making as the children played their part, which, as above intimated was a leading one. There was something singularly appropriate about the selection of the cantata which was presented—" Spring triumphant." In this,Spring's rightmaf place is contested by grim-visaged Winter and the warmer season emerges triumphant. Earlier in the day a slight fall of snow and the presence of a cold and boisterous wind showed that in reality winter was , reluctant to take his departure and many were the hopes expressed that the next few day would see Spring triumph as completeiy as she did at the, bazaar. The chorus of the cantata was composed of children and they having taken up their appointed stations on the platform a number of daintily attired maidens tripped lightly on to the stage and made merry whilst they awaited the advent of their Queen. Her arrival (as Spring) is the signal for continuance of the revels with even greater vigour and the rejoicing is at its height when Winter, in the garb of Father Christmas, and his snow-clad attendants, enters and endeavours to drive Her away. Two attempts to achieve this object prove unsuccessful and in the end he has to acknow- ledge defeat and retires to the regions of perpetual snow. Spring is crowned with flowers and after merry- making she and her. subjects- take a fond farewell and depart? The whole scene was prettily enacted. The children threw themselves into the piece with great I enthusiasm and their singing and dancing was very effective, an animated and picturesque scene being pre- sented. Spring (Miss Lena Duckworth) comported herself with a dignity which befitted her position and sang with great sweetness and an appreciation of the spirit of the music. Master T.Allender portrayed Winter land was also a great success, singing well, whilst his acting was also realistic. Great pains had been taken to prepare the children who combined well in the choruses and performed the evolutions of intricate dances with precision and great charm.. Their 'efforts were most enthusiastically applauded and a suggestion that the performance should be repeated ere the close of the bazaar was heartily seconded by the audience. The arrangements were in the hands of the following committee :—Mr. W. Silcock, Mrs. Dakin Misses L. Bayley, Collin, E. Jones, R. Osborne, Sterling, E. Taylor' Emily -Taylor, and M. Wilde To much praise cannot be given to Mr. Baxter for his work in preparing the children. The musical portion of the cantata was supplied by a ladies orchestra consisting of the Misses G.Henshall,E. Taylor, Tunstall, E. Osborne, E. Ward, Sterling, A. Birtles, and F. Bennett, and whose work was deserving of the warmest praise. At the conclusion of the performance Miss Dutton presented Miss Duckworth with a beautiful bouquet. The next item was the presentation of purses by the children, containing sums which they had collected, and these were handed to Mrs. Reginald Silcook, vflio afterwards briefly declared the bazaar open. She and Mrs. Roberts were made the recipients of handsome bouquets, which were handed to them b 3, two tiny dots, Miss D. Lingard and Master T. Weaver. A vote of thanks was accorded to both ladies, on the motion of Miss BESSIE DUTTON, seconded by Mrs. WITHINSII&W, and the sales for the day commenced. Thursday's sales realized £255. YESTERDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

There was again a large attendance at the opening ceremony, which was performed 'by Mr. John Taylor. Mr. R. H. Meldrum presided and there were present: —Revs. S. Pitt, J. E. Smith, J. Manning, W. Mellor, Mrs. Taylor, Mrs. Meldrum, Messrs. H. W. Guest, F. A. Harris, I. E. Barlow J. E. Holland, R. H. Silcock, E. Plinston, and Mr. and Mrs. R. H. Silcock. _

The Rev. S. Prrr at the outset congratulated them upon the success of the bazaar, a success which had been thoroughly provided for and deserved. He was very much inclined to think that they would create a record in Warrington bazaars. (Applause). The CHAIRMAN said some had been apt to regard Bold-street Church as past its prime, but he thought that they had shown that Bold-street not only had been but was vigorous. (Applause.) Mr. TAYLOR briefly declared the bazaar open, speak- in high praise of the activity which had been shown and of the excellent work done by the Rev. J. Manning. Votes of thanks were accorded to the two gentlemen for their services on the motion of Mr. F. A. Harris, seconded by Mr. J. C. Holland.

TO-DAY'S OPENING.

The bazaar will be continued to-day and will be opened at 3.30. The principal feature will be the reproduction ot the cantata "Spring Triumphant."

THE PROCEEDS.

Yesterdays proceeds were £257 17s lid making a total amount of £1461 17 Id. THE ENTERTAINMENTS.

The series of entertainments has been one of the most admirable ever arranged for a local bazaar. Special praise is due to Mr. Arthur Down for bringing his military band and orchestra which, have delighted the visitors with choice selections. Each evening there were side shows of a particu- larly entertaining character. On Tuesday, Wednesday, and Thursday, high-class concerts attracted large audi- ences, the artistes being Miss Gertrude Henshall, Miss E. Taylor, Hilda Ashcroft (aged four years), Mrs. A. Booth, Miss Marie Houghton, Miss Armstrong, Miss Thompson, Miss Clatworthy, Miss Elsie Taylor' Miss Mabel Henshall, Miss Carrie Hastings, Mr. J. W. Bibby, Mr. D. Gittens, Apollo Prize Quartette, Mr. J. L. Davenport, Mr. Ernest Sanders. Excellent programmes were submitted, including songs, humorous recitals, banjo, mandoline and violin solos, and gave the greatest pleasure. The concerts were arranged by Mr. Frank Hickman, Mr. E. Plinston and Kr. Alfred J. Silcock. Mr. Harold A. Silcock was the accompanist. Each evening a humorous sketch was given and created great amusement. The characters were sustained by the following :—" April Fools " (by W. F. Chapman), Mr. Peter Brown, Mr. Hedley H. Taylor ; Mr. James Smith, Mr. W. Johnson; Mr. Joseph Smith, Mr. E. J. Sutton. "To be Let" (by W. H. Goldsmith), Fred Markham, Mr. Hedley H. Taylor : Dora Dixon, Miss Marian Ellison. " The Hard-up Rivals" (by J. L. Hillcox), Mackintosh Muffin, Mr. E. J. Sutton; Petrucio Puffin, Mr. Hedley EL Taylor; Timms (the waiter), Mr. W. Johnson. The exhibitions of tableaux vivants, arranged by Mr. and Mrs. J. Baxter, proved an exceedingly popular feature. The children's variety concerts by Mr. T. Brough's Harmonic Choir and Mr. J. Baxter's Secondary School Party were most creditable. Other attractions included the Royal Court marionettes, and "Professor Marvello's " conjuring feats. There were also a miniature rifle range and shooting jungle under the charge of Mr. G. Ashton, grand prize competitions, a fish pond, and fairy well under the charge of the Misses E. Cooper, K. Hayward, N. Kershaw, S. Ostle, R. Osborne, M. Parry, E. Plinston, and L. Walker, and a palmistry tent. The bazaar book of recipes and quotations, constituting a unique souvenir, had been compiled by Mrs. Percy Silcock and Mr. G. W. Weaver. There was also a bazaar bank, the treasurers of which were Mr. J. C. Holland and Mr. James Parkinson.

Services for Sunday.

Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church : Special services will be held in celebration of the anniversary of the Silver-street Sunday School. The preacher for the day will be the Rev. J. Grange Bennett. A choral service will he held in the afternoon when Mr. Upson will preside and an address will be delivered by Dr. Ferguson. Wycliffe Congregational Church: Preacher, 10.30 and 6.30, Rev. J. Yonge. Golborne-street Baptist Church : Preacher, 10.30 and 6.30, Rev. Edward Moore, Leeds. Bold-street Wesleyan Church : Preachers, 10.30, Rev. E. J. Gawne ; 6.30, Rev. S. Pitt.

Drowning Mystery. Mr. S. Brighouse, coroner, held an inquest at Sankey, Warrington, vester lay afternoon in respect to the death of Jane Alice Guest, aged 34, of 217, Winwick-road, Warrington, whose body was found in the Sankey and St. Helens Canal, near Bewsey, on Tuesday morning - The husband of the deceased stated that during the last twelve months his wife gave way to excessive drinking. He remonstrated with her about her conduct, and on this account he refused to give her his wages. She was drinking heavily on Monday, and on Tuesday morning about nine o'clock she left home, stating she was going for a walk. Later in the morning her body was recovered from the canal by a waterman named Bate. The jury returned an open verdict.

THE BEST YOU CAN BUY.

Dried currants, when suitably cooked, represent the highest attainment in healthful and nourishing food. They cleanse and enrich the blood, and are nourishing to an extent seldom realised by those who eat and enjoy them. Such facts are surely sufficient to encour- age the housemother to a wise generosity in the use of a food which is always easily available and which costs only a few ponce per pound.

FINANCIAL.

Particulars of ENGLAND'S PREMIER COBALT MINING COMPANY (LTD)., have now been issued for public information only. In connection with the Cobalt District of Ontario, Canada, the Canadian Premier, Sir Wilfred Laurier, stated in an interview with a contemporary last week that " I have no personal knowledge of it, but I really believe thtt the Cobalt District is fabulously rich ; indeed, I know it. The richness of this district has never been equalled in regard to the deposits of sil.ver, not even in Colorando or Nevada. So far, however, as I know the Ontario Government has taken prompt and energetic action, which, in ray opinion, is both wise and timely in regard to extra precaution so that no conflict of title is possible ; while sound security of tenure is assured The Government of the Province. causes careful examination by its own mining experts to be made, and until an official investigation is -completed,and the Government Inspector is satisfied •that the property in question possesses payable. mineral in place, no titles are granted." ENGLAND'S PREMIER COBALT MINING COMPANY (LTD.), is in close proximity to sucfi well known mines as the Buffalo, Silver Queen, Hudson ! Bay, and Tretheway properties, which have shipped some of the richest ore in the Cobalt- region. ENGLAND'S PREMIER COBALr MINING " COM- PANY (LTD.) has recently been formed with a capital of £220,000 ; ample working capital has been guaranteed, and the average assays of ore are reported to run from 300 to 900 ounces of silver per ton (exclusive of other minerals). Within a few months shipments are expected to be made, and the shares are being freely dealt in on the London Stock Exchange. The Offices of the Company are at Trafalgar Buildings, Northumberland Avenue, London, W.C.

Two youna fishermen of Cromer were drowned about a mile off that town in a squall on Thursday afternoon. On the flues of the Knutsford celebration a gorgeous May Day Festival took place on Thursday, at Ramsey, Isle of Man. The May Queen. a pretty girl, was crowned with due pomp, and in the eveaing she attended a brilliant function, with her rttinue, at the pavilion.

The soloists were Mr. Arthur Griffiths and Mr. N. Cook (tenors) and ; . ot Mr. B. Hibbert (bass), while a-quartette was given by the Misses Griffiths and Armstrong, Messrs. Cook and Barnes. The service was presided over by Mr. W. IL Sallington and the Rev. Frank Vaughan delivered an address on the value and importance of Sunday school work. A cordial vote of thanks was accorded to Mrs. Brown and Mr. Payton on the proposing of Mr. Eccles seconded by Mr. Beswick. , In the evening the Rev. DAVID WALTEnS preached on the words: "And we know that to them that love God all things work together for good" (Romans 8, 28). He said that was one of the most audacious assertions that human imagination could conceive and the question which faced them was "Is it true ? " Could they literally believe and act on a statement of that kind— that all things in every place; at all time, under all Icircumstances, without any exception worked together for good? Their Suitt inquiry must be what it meant by ' good." To many people good meant ease, com- fort and indulgence, or anything which gave them thair own way, but if the text meant that then they knew it was not true. If filling their pockets meant emptying their heads were they satisfied with the bargain? Would they call that working together for good? He could do no better than quote the answer of Sir Oliver Lodge who said "Good was that which promoted development, was in parallel with the will of God." That which made for their good must be for their development. His (the speaker's) next difficulty about the Christian religion was that it was too good to be true, and he was in tile same difficulty with regard to his text. The more they recognised the rights of their fellow men, the more individual they became. Their loneliness was one aspect of it. The great question was therefore: did they love God or did they believe the world? If they became like the world they might depend upon it—it mattered not whether they prospered. it would only make them more worldly. If they loved God, they knew that all things, peace or war, pleasure or sorrow, success or failure, gain or loss, ministered for the development of that character which was for ultimate and eternal good. They were not pledged to understand or explain some great doctrine in order to follow Jesus Christ. The test of Christianity was not a dogmatic faith. The service opened impressively with the anthem "I waited for the Lord" (Mendelssohn), in which a duet was sung by Miss Marie Houghton and Miss E. Knowles. Later the choir sang effectively "Hear my prayer" (Mendelssobn) Miss Marie Houghton's render- ing of the solo being most expressive and artistic. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., able presided at the organ throughout the day and to him the success of the musical parts of the services was in a great measure due. The collections totalled over £4O.
